# Mingle

**Mingle** means to **move around and talk to different people in a social setting**. It’s about mixing with others and having casual conversations, instead of sticking with just one group.

### **Quick Examples**

* *“At the party, everyone was mingling and chatting.”*

* *“I don’t know many people here, but I’ll try to mingle.”*

* *“She likes to mingle with colleagues after work.”*

### **Sentences You Can Use**

1. “I’ll mingle a bit and then come back.”

2. “He mingled with the guests during the reception.”

3. “It’s a good chance to mingle and make connections.”

4. “I’m not great at mingling, but I’ll give it a try.”

💡 **Context of usage**:

* **Casual/social**: parties, weddings, networking events.

* **Professional**: company gatherings, conferences (often said as *“network and mingle”*).

👉 Formal alternatives: **socialize, network, interact**.

✨ Practice aloud: *“I’ll mingle with the team after the meeting.”*

### **Literal Meanings of *Mingle***

1. **To mix or blend substances**

* *“The smell of coffee mingled with fresh bread.”*

* *“Tears mingled with her laughter.”*

* *“His excitement mingled with nervousness.”* (here it’s feelings, but still a mix)

2. **To join or combine**

* *“Streams mingled as they flowed into the river.”*

* *“The colors mingled beautifully on the canvas.”*

1. “The aroma of spices mingled in the air.”

2. “Rain mingled with dust on the ground.”

3. “Music mingled with laughter during the festival.”

💡 **So, summary**:

* **Social sense** → moving around and mixing with people.

* **Literal sense** → different things (sounds, smells, colors, feelings, objects) mixing together.

👉 Practice aloud: *“The smell of rain mingled with the cool breeze.”*

Do you want me to also give you a few **metaphorical uses** (like “cultures mingling” or “traditions mingling”) that are common in professional or thoughtful discussions?

# Molasses

Great word\! **Molasses** is thick, dark syrup that comes from refining sugarcane or sugar beets. It’s sticky, very sweet, and pours very slowly.

### **Meanings & Usage:**

1. **Literal (food)**

   * Used in baking, cooking, or making rum.

   * *“This cake has a rich flavor because of the molasses.”*

2. **Figurative (metaphor)**

   * Because molasses is **slow and heavy**, people use it to describe slowness.

   * *“Traffic is moving like molasses today.”* (very slow)

   * *“My computer runs slower than molasses.”*

3. **Emotional/metaphorical tone**

   * Sometimes it implies heaviness or difficulty.

   * *“Getting him out of bed in the morning is like pulling molasses uphill.”*

✅ Sentences you can practice:

1. *“The project moved forward as slowly as molasses.”*

2. *“After the update, my laptop felt like molasses.”*

3. *“He speaks so slowly, almost like molasses dripping.”*

4. *“This syrup is thick, almost like molasses.”*

👉 Notice how it’s often used in similes: *“like molasses”* to stress slowness.

💡 Context of usage:

* In **casual talk** → slowness (“internet is slower than molasses”).

* In **work talk** → performance issues (“our job execution is crawling like molasses”).

* In **cooking talk** → actual ingredient.

# Moment of Truth

The phrase **“moment of truth”** means the point in time when something important is tested, revealed, or decided. It’s that critical moment when you find out whether something works, succeeds, or fails.

For example:

* In sports, the penalty kick in the final seconds can be called *the moment of truth*.

* In work, when you finally present your solution to the client, that’s *the moment of truth*.

* In personal life, confessing your feelings to someone can also feel like *the moment of truth*.

It usually carries a sense of pressure and finality—where preparation ends, and reality shows the result.

✅ Here are some **spoken phrases** you could use in real life:

1. **“This is the moment of truth—let’s see if our hard work pays off.”**

2. **“We’ve done all the preparation; now comes the moment of truth.”**

3. **“It’s the moment of truth for our project presentation.”**

4. **“The moment of truth will show whether the system can handle real traffic.”**

[↑ Back to index](#index)

# Move Around

### **1\. To Change Locations Frequently**

* **Meaning**: To go from one place to another often.  
* **Example**: As a child, I moved around a lot because my dad was in the army.  
* **Example**: Tourists love to move around the city, exploring new places.

### **2\. To Shift Position or Be Active**

* **Meaning**: To not stay in one place; to walk, run, or change posture.  
* **Example**: Stop moving around so much\! Sit still for a moment.  
* **Example**: After sitting for hours, it's good to move around to stretch your legs.

### **3\. To Rearrange Things**

* **Meaning**: To change the placement of objects.  
* **Example**: I moved around the furniture to make the living room look bigger.  
* **Example**: He keeps moving around his desk setup to find the perfect spot.

### **4\. To Change Plans or Adjust Schedules**

* **Meaning**: To modify arrangements or reschedule something.  
* **Example**: Can we move around the meeting to a later time?  
* **Example**: My plans keep moving around because of last-minute work changes.

# Move past it

"Move past it" means to recover from something by intentionally forgetting or overcoming it. It can also mean to get ahead of something or to move or proceed with a given movement or momentum.   
For example, "Nobody likes to be rejected, but I got past it".

# moving forward

### **✅ Meaning of “moving forward”**

The phrase **“moving forward”** means *from this point on* or *in the future*.  
 It’s often used in **spoken English** to shift focus toward **what will happen next** or **what should be done next** — especially in meetings or discussions.

### **✅ Examples in spoken English**

1. **“Moving forward, we should document all our deployments.”**  
    → (From now on, we’ll do this every time.)

2. **“Let’s try to improve our testing strategy moving forward.”**  
    → (In the future, we should make it better.)

3. **“Moving forward, we need to avoid the same mistake.”**  
    → (Going ahead, let’s not repeat it.)

4. **“I think moving forward, we’ll face some new challenges.”**  
    → (In the future, there may be new issues.)

### **⚠️ Your example:**

“Moving forward in further we can get this as issue as well.”

That sentence is understandable but **not natural**.  
 Here are better ways to say it:

* ✅ “Moving forward, we might face this issue again.”

* ✅ “We could run into this issue again moving forward.”

* ✅ “This could become a recurring issue moving forward.”

### **💬 Common spoken phrases you can practice:**

1. “Moving forward, I suggest we keep things more transparent.”

2. “That being said, we’ll have to monitor this closely moving forward.”

3. “On the other hand, moving forward, we can plan better to avoid it.”

4. “For example, if this happens again moving forward, we’ll be prepared.”

5. “So, moving forward, let’s make sure we’re aligned on this.”

[↑ Back to index](#index)

# Needle back

**“Needle back”** is usually used in the context of **responding to someone who is teasing, provoking, or criticizing you**. It comes from the verb **“needle”**, which means **to annoy, provoke, or goad someone deliberately**.

### **Meaning:**

* **Needle back \= respond in kind to someone who is needling you.**

* Often playful, sometimes defensive, depending on tone.

### **Usage in Spoken English:**

1. **Playful teasing:**

   * *“He kept making jokes about my cooking, so I needled back about his messy kitchen.”*

2. **Friendly banter:**

   * *“She needled me about my new haircut, and I needled back about her fashion sense.”*

3. **Figurative / mild conflict:**

   * *“The two politicians needled back and forth during the debate.”*

💡 **Tip for speaking:**

* “Needling” is more **informal** and **conversational**, often used in **friendly teasing** or **light arguments**.

* You can say: *“I couldn’t help needling back”* or *“He needled me, so I needled back.”*

# Nip

### **1\. To bite, pinch, or squeeze lightly**

* *“The dog nipped my hand.”* (bit lightly)

* *“The baby kept nipping my arm.”* (pinching playfully)

### **2\. To cut off or stop something quickly (often in the phrase *“nip in the bud”*)**

👉 *“Nip in the bud”* means to stop a problem early before it grows bigger.

* *“We need to nip this bug in the bud before it affects production.”*

* *“If you see negativity in the team, nip it in the bud.”*

### **3\. To go quickly (British informal)**

* *“I’ll just nip to the store.”* (I’ll quickly go to the store)

* *“She nipped out for a coffee.”*

### **4\. A small amount of alcohol (informal)**

* *“He had a quick nip of whiskey.”*

### **Spoken Sentences You Can Use**

1. “We should nip this issue in the bud before it spreads.”

2. “I’m going to nip out for five minutes.”

3. “The dog nipped my finger, but it wasn’t serious.”

4. “Let’s nip that rumor in the bud before it gets out of hand.”

💡 **Context of usage**:

* In **professional meetings**, you’ll mainly hear *“nip it in the bud”*.

* In **casual British English**, “nip out” or “nip to” is very common for quick errands.

* “Nip” for biting or drinking is usually casual/personal.

# Nothing short of extraordinary

The phrase "nothing short of extraordinary" means something is absolutely remarkable, exceptional, or amazing—it’s as good as extraordinary can get and not lacking in any way. Essentially, it emphasizes that whatever is being described is far beyond ordinary or average.

Examples:  
"The hospitality at the hotel was nothing short of extraordinary."  
(The hospitality was exceptional, far better than expected.)

(Her recovery was incredible and remarkable.)

It’s a way to strongly praise or highlight how impressive something or someone is.

# Nowhere

* **Basic meaning:** *not in or to any place*.  
   Example: *“I looked for my keys everywhere, but they were nowhere to be found.”*

* **Figurative meaning:** *no progress / no importance / no chance*.  
   Example: *“This project is going nowhere.”*

### **Phrase: nowhere close to**

This means *“not at all near to”* or *“far from being”*.

* *“That explanation is nowhere close to correct.”* (It’s totally wrong.)

* *“We’re nowhere close to finishing the work.”* (We still have a long way to go.)

It’s often used for **emphasis** when something is very far from the truth, a goal, or an expectation.

### **Similar words/phrases and usage**

1. **Far from**

   * *“This solution is far from perfect.”*  
      (Meaning: it’s not perfect at all.)

2. **Not even near**

   * *“The quality is not even near what we expected.”*

3. **Nowhere near**

   * This is almost the same as “nowhere close to.”

   * *“The stadium was nowhere near full.”*

4. **Miles away from** *(informal)*

   * *“Your guess is miles away from the real answer.”*

### **Common spoken sentences you can practice:**

1. *“We’re nowhere close to finishing this project.”*

2. *“That’s nowhere near what I meant.”*

3. *“Your estimate is far from accurate.”*

4. *“The result is not even close to what we expected.”*

👉 Try saying them aloud slowly, then a bit faster, to get the rhythm.

# Off guard

### **Off guard — Meaning**

**“Off guard”** means **being surprised or unprepared for something**.  
 When someone is *caught off guard*, it means they **did not expect the situation and were not ready to respond immediately**.

So, the idea is: **you were not mentally prepared.**

For example:  
 If someone suddenly asks you a difficult question in a meeting and you were not expecting it, you might feel **caught off guard**.

### **Simple Examples (Real-life usage)**

1. **Meeting / Work**  
* *“The client asked a question about pricing and it caught me off guard.”*  
2. **Conversation**  
* *“His comment really caught me off guard. I didn’t know how to respond.”*  
3. **Unexpected situation**  
* *“The sudden change in the plan caught everyone off guard.”*  
4. **Personal reaction**  
* *“I was caught off guard when she mentioned my name in the presentation.”*

### **Important Phrase**

The most common expression is:

**“caught off guard”**

* *“I was caught off guard by that question.”*

### **Comparison with similar words**

| Phrase | Meaning |
| ----- | ----- |
| **Off guard** | Not prepared |
| **Surprised** | Unexpected reaction |
| **Taken aback** | Emotionally surprised |
| **Caught off guard** | Suddenly unprepared |

Example difference:

* *“I was surprised by the question.”*  
* *“I was caught off guard by the question.”* → stronger; shows **lack of preparation**

### **Useful Spoken Sentences (Practice for meetings)**

Try speaking these aloud:

1. **“That question caught me off guard, but let me think about it for a moment.”**  
2. **“To be honest, that came a bit out of nowhere and caught me off guard.”**  
3. **“Initially it caught me off guard; however, after thinking about it, the solution became clear.”**  
4. **“For example, when unexpected questions come in a meeting, they can sometimes catch you off guard.”**  
5. **“That being said, it’s always good to pause and organize your thoughts before answering.”**

### **How you might use it in your real scenario**

Since you mentioned earlier that **sometimes in client calls you struggle when unexpected questions come**, you could say something like:

*“That question caught me slightly off guard. Let me quickly walk through the architecture and explain how it works.”*

This sounds **natural and professional in meetings**.

### **Quick Speaking Practice**

Say this sentence aloud 3 times:

**“The question caught me off guard, but let me think through it and explain.”**

This will help you sound **calm and confident even when surprised**.

If you'd like, I can also show you **5 advanced phrases similar to “caught off guard” that sound very natural in meetings.** (These are extremely useful in professional conversations.)

# off the top of one's head

phrase of head  
without careful thought or investigation.

(meaning: without checking or confirming details).

Describes something physically on or near the top of one’s head.

# Off-putting

“Off-putting” means something that makes you feel uncomfortable, unpleasant, or discouraged. It’s usually used to describe behavior, appearance, tone, or situations that give you a negative impression.

For example:

* A strong unpleasant smell can be *off-putting*.

* If someone speaks very rudely in a meeting, their attitude might feel *off-putting*.

* Complicated instructions in a tool could be *off-putting* for new users.

### **Common spoken sentences:**

1. “I found his tone quite off-putting, to be honest.”

2. “The smell in the room was a bit off-putting.”

3. “Her comment was off-putting, but I tried not to take it personally.”

4. “The user interface looks off-putting at first, but it gets easier once you try it.”

👉 You can use “off-putting” in both formal (work, presentations) and informal (casual chat) settings.

# On and on

The phrase **“on and on”** means *something continues without stopping, often for a long time, and sometimes in an annoying or boring way*.  
 It’s commonly used in spoken English when describing:

* Speech that doesn’t stop

* Actions or processes that keep repeating

* Time that feels endless

**Examples in Spoken English**

1. **About talking:**

   * *“He went on and on about his new car, and honestly, it got a bit tiring.”*  
      (Here, it means he kept talking too much.)

2. **About time/duration:**

   * *“The meeting went on and on, and we were all waiting for it to finish.”*  
      (The meeting lasted much longer than expected.)

3. **About effort or action:**

   * *“She kept working on and on until the project was finally done.”*  
      (She continued without stopping.)

Connected Sentences (how to link smoothly)

* *“The lecture went on and on, **and to make matters worse**, the speaker didn’t even take questions.”*

* *“He kept talking on and on about his vacation, **but at the same time**, it was nice to see his excitement.”*

* *“The rain went on and on, **so eventually**, the match had to be canceled.”*

Phrases you can practice saying in meetings or casual talk

* “This discussion can’t just go on and on, we need to decide.”

* “The issue has dragged on and on without resolution.”

* “She explained the details on and on, until everyone understood.”

* “It felt like the movie just went on and on.”

[↑ Back to index](#index)

# On Him

### **1\. “On him / on her” with phrasal verbs**

* Many English verbs take a preposition, and the preposition cannot be dropped.

* For example:

  * *“I count on him.”* → means *I rely on him.*

  * *“She depends on her.”* → means *She trusts her for support.*

Here, **“on” is part of the verb (count on / depend on)**, and “him/her” is the object.

### **2\. When “on” shows direction / position**

Sometimes “on him / on her” literally means physically “on top of” or “attached to”:

* *“The jacket looks good on him.”* → The jacket suits him.

* *“The baby is sleeping on her.”* → Baby is lying on her body.

### **3\. When it shows responsibility / blame**

* *“That’s on him.”* → It means *That’s his responsibility or his fault.*

* *“Don’t blame me, it’s on her.”* → The fault is hers.

✅ **So, the pattern is not a special grammar rule by itself**. It usually comes from either:

* A phrasal verb (*count on, rely on, depend on*),

* A descriptive phrase (*looks good on him*),

* Or an idiom (*that’s on him \= his responsibility*).

### **Phrases you can practice saying:**

1. *“You can always count on me.”* (phrasal verb use)

2. *“That shirt looks perfect on her.”* (appearance use)

3. *“If the project fails, that’s on him.”* (responsibility use)

4. *“She’s depending on him to finish the work.”* (trust/support use)  
5. “He doesn’t have a keyboard on him.”  
6. *“Do you have your ID on you?”* (Do you have it with you?)

7. *“She didn’t have any cash on her.”* (She wasn’t carrying cash.)

8. *“They don’t have their tickets on them.”* (They don’t have the tickets with them right now.)  
9. *“Sorry, I don’t have any money on me right now.”*

10. *“Do you have a pen on you?”*

11. *“He didn’t have his phone on him when I called.”*

12. *“She always has her keys on her.”*  
13. *“He gave me feedback **on** my presentation.”* → feedback **about** the presentation.

14. *“She wrote a review **on** the new book.”* → review **about** the book.

15. *“Can you give me comments **on** my report?”* → comments **about** the report.

16. *“The manager’s remarks **on** the project were helpful.”* → remarks **about** the project.

    ### **💡 Compare with other prepositions:**

* **“On”** → about the topic. (*review on the movie*)

* **“About”** → also works, but **“on” sounds more formal and natural** in work/academic settings.

* **“For”** → different meaning. *“Feedback for me”* \= directed to me, not necessarily about my work.

  ### **Spoken phrases you can practice:**

1. *“Can you share your comments **on** this document?”*

2. *“I’d like to get your feedback **on** my code.”*

3. *“The client left a review **on** our website.”*

4. *“He gave some helpful notes **on** my presentation.”*

# on the flip side

The phrase **“on the flip side”** is a casual, conversational way to say **“on the other hand”** or **“from another perspective.”** It’s used when you want to show contrast or present a different point of view.

### **Usage in Spoken English:**

1. **To show the opposite side of an argument or situation:**

   * *“I love living in the city. On the flip side, it’s really noisy.”*

   * *“Working from home is convenient. On the flip side, it can be lonely.”*

2. **To give a contrasting result or consequence:**

   * *“The new policy saves money. On the flip side, it makes employees’ jobs harder.”*

3. **To soften disagreement or criticism:**

   * *“Your idea is interesting. On the flip side, we might need to consider the costs.”*

💡 **Tip:**

* It’s **informal**, so you’ll mostly hear it in **conversation**, not formal writing.

* Can be swapped with **“however”**, **“alternatively”**, or **“but”**, depending on context.

# Out of the way

The phrase "out of the way" can have several meanings depending on the context. Here are the most common ones:

**Not obstructing or blocking:**

Something or someone is no longer in a position that causes an obstacle.  
Example: "He moved the chair out of the way so I could pass."

**Completed or dealt with:**

A task or responsibility has been finished or resolved.  
Example: "Let’s get this project out of the way before starting a new one."

**In a remote or isolated location:**

Refers to a place that is not easily accessible or is far from the main areas.  
Example: "Their house is in a quiet, out-of-the-way village."

**Unusual or extraordinary (less common usage):**

Refers to something that is not normal or expected.  
Example: "It was out of the way for him to act so kindly."

# Over the top

"A little over the top" is an idiom that means something is slightly too extreme or excessive for the situation. For example, you might describe a birthday party with clowns, ponies, and a chocolate fountain as "over the top". 

Examples 

"I thought her performance was a little over the top".

"The decorations were a little over the top".

"The speech was a bit OTT".

Synonyms for "over the top": 

excessive, extravagant, extreme, insane, inappropriate, unbearable, flamboyant, outrageous, ridiculous, and unbelievable.

**"Over the top"** means **excessive**, **exaggerated**, or **beyond what’s reasonable or normal**.  
 It can describe behavior, emotions, style, or actions — sometimes in a negative way (too much) and sometimes in a playful or admiring way (dramatic flair).

1. **Negative / Excessive**

   * *"His reaction to the mistake was over the top — he yelled for an hour."*

   * *"The decorations were beautiful but a bit over the top for a small party."*

2. **Positive / Playful or Dramatic**

   * *"The performance was over the top, but that’s what made it so entertaining."*

   * *"She wore an over-the-top costume for Halloween and stole the show."*

### **🔸 Synonyms:**

* Excessive

* Exaggerated

* Extreme

* Dramatic

💡 In **military history**, "over the top" originally meant soldiers going out of their trenches to attack — but in everyday English today, it’s almost always about **exaggeration**.

# Oversee

The verb **“oversee”** means:

👉 *to supervise, manage, or watch over something to make sure it is done correctly.*

It comes from “over” (above) \+ “see” (to look), so it literally means *“to look over from above.”*

### **Usage examples**

1. *“The manager will oversee the project to ensure everything runs smoothly.”*

2. *“Teachers oversee students during examinations to prevent cheating.”*

3. *“She was hired to oversee the daily operations of the company.”*

🔹 **Synonyms:** supervise, manage, monitor, direct, administer, control.

# paint the picture

The phrase **“paint the picture”** is an idiom. It doesn’t mean to literally paint with colors—it means to **describe something clearly and vividly so that the listener can imagine it in their mind**.

For example, in a meeting, your manager might say:  
 👉 *“Can you paint the picture for us about where the project stands right now?”*  
 That means they want you to explain the situation in detail so everyone can see the “whole scene” mentally.

It’s often used in business, storytelling, or personal conversations when someone wants a fuller explanation instead of short facts.

### **Useful sentences you can practice:**

1. *“Let me paint the picture for you—this is where we started and this is where we are now.”*

2. *“To paint the picture more clearly, imagine if…”*

3. *“Before I explain the solution, I’ll paint the picture of the problem first.”*

4. *“That paints a pretty good picture of the challenges we’re facing.”*

💡 **Context of usage**:

* In **meetings** (explaining status or challenges).

* In **presentations** (helping the audience visualize data or a scenario).

* In **casual talk** (telling a story, describing an event, setting the scene).

# Pan out

**"Pan out"** means *to turn out well* or *to happen in a particular way*, especially when talking about the outcome of plans, ideas, or efforts.

**Examples:**

* *We’ll see how the project pans out before making a decision.*

* *His risky investment didn’t pan out.* (it didn’t succeed)

* *I hope things pan out for you in your new job.*

**Origin:**  
 It comes from gold prospecting — miners would swirl a pan of river sediment hoping to find gold. If the gold appeared, the effort “panned out” successfully.

It’s mostly used in **informal spoken English** today.

To turn out or happen successfully.  
 👉 *“Let’s see how this plan pans out.”*

# Pent up

“**Pent up**” means something that has been **held inside or suppressed for a long time**, usually emotions, energy, or feelings, and then it either builds pressure or comes out strongly later.

* If someone has **pent-up anger**, it means they’ve been holding their anger in without expressing it.

* If you have **pent-up energy**, it means you’ve been sitting idle and now you feel restless and want to move.

It comes from the idea of something being “penned up” (like animals in a pen) — trapped and unable to get out.

✅ **Examples in sentences (spoken style):**

1. “He finally shouted after days of keeping his **pent-up frustration** inside.”

2. “Kids have so much **pent-up energy** after sitting in class all day.”

3. “You could tell she had **pent-up emotions**—once she started talking, she just couldn’t stop.”

4. “I think I need to go for a run; I’ve got a lot of **pent-up energy**.”

💡 **Where you can use it:**

* In **work meetings**, if talking about stress: *“We shouldn’t let **pent-up frustration** affect the team.”*

* In **casual talk**, about kids or friends: *“They had so much **pent-up energy** after being indoors.”*

* In **personal reflection**, about emotions: *“I realized I had a lot of **pent-up feelings** I needed to express.”*

👉 Try speaking aloud:

* “I think I have a lot of pent-up stress these days.”

* “After the exam, all my pent-up tension just disappeared.”

[↑ Back to index](#index)

# Pent-up

**Pent-up** describes **feelings, energy, or emotions** that have been **kept inside or restrained for a long time**, often building pressure until they are released.

### **🔸 Definition:**

Stored or suppressed over time, unable to be freely expressed.

### **🔸 Examples:**

1. **Emotions**

   * *"She finally let out her pent-up frustration."*

   * *"Years of pent-up anger exploded in that moment."*

2. **Energy / Urges**

   * *"The kids had a lot of pent-up energy after being indoors all day."*

   * *"He released his pent-up creativity through painting."*

### **🔸 Synonyms:**

* Repressed

* Bottled-up

* Suppressed

* Held back

💡 Often used with **anger**, **frustration**, **energy**, **demand**, or **emotion**.

Think of it like a **pressure cooker** — the more it’s held in, the stronger the release.

# Pivot away

**"Pivot away"** is a common phrase in spoken (and written) English that means **to change direction, focus, or strategy**—especially when moving away from a current topic, plan, or approach.

### **🔹 Meaning**

To "pivot away" from something means to deliberately **stop focusing on one thing and move toward something else**—just like how a basketball player pivots by shifting one foot to turn in a different direction.

### **🔹 Usage in Spoken English**

#### **1\. In Business/Tech**

🔸 *“We had to pivot away from our initial product idea after customer feedback showed it wasn't solving a real problem.”*

🔸 *“They’re pivoting away from on-premise infrastructure and moving to the cloud.”*

#### **2\. In Conversations/Presentations**

🔸 *“Let’s pivot away from the technical details and focus on the business impact.”*

🔸 *“Before we wrap up, I want to pivot away from architecture and touch on the deployment challenges.”*

#### **3\. In Politics/Media**

🔸 *“The candidate tried to pivot away from the question about taxes.”*

🔸 *“He smoothly pivoted away from the controversy by highlighting his future plans.”*

### **🔹 Similar Phrases**

* Shift focus from

* Move away from

* Transition away from

* Change direction

### **🔹 Example (Tech Team Discussion)**

*“We initially used RNNs for the time series model, but due to poor performance, we pivoted away from them and adopted a transformer-based approach.”*

# Pontification

### **Meaning of pontification**

**Pontification** means **speaking in a way that sounds too serious, too confident, or like you believe you are always right—often without inviting discussion**.

It sometimes has a slightly **negative tone**, especially in meetings or technical discussions.

**Simple explanation:**  
 When someone talks as if they are the final authority and doesn’t consider other opinions, they are *pontificating*.

**Example:**

He kept pontificating about architecture decisions without listening to the team.

In workplace settings (especially architecture or MLOps discussions), this word is useful when describing communication style.

### **Where you can use it (technical context) 💻**

As a Cloud Architect or MLOps Engineer, you might use it when discussing collaboration styles:

* design reviews  
* architecture meetings  
* stakeholder discussions  
* leadership communication feedback

Instead of pontificating, we should open this for discussion across teams.

This sounds mature and diplomatic.

### **Natural spoken sentences you can use 🎯**

Practice saying these aloud:

1. “I don’t want to sound like I’m pontificating, but I think we should reconsider this approach.”  
2. “Sometimes in architecture meetings, people start pontificating instead of collaborating.”  
3. “That being said, let’s avoid pontificating and gather feedback from the team.”  
4. “For example, we can present options instead of pontificating about a single solution.”  
5. “On the other hand, a little confidence is good—as long as it doesn’t become pontification.”

### **Related useful alternatives (more common in meetings)**

These sound more natural in everyday corporate conversation:

* “being too opinionated”  
* “speaking authoritatively”  
* “talking at people instead of with people”  
* “pushing a viewpoint strongly”

I don’t want to be too opinionated here, but this architecture might not scale well.

### **Gentle correction of your word ✏️**

You wrote: **ponitification**  
 Correct spelling: **pontification**

A natural way to ask your question would be:

What does pontification mean, and how do we use it in conversation?

Try speaking one sentence aloud now:

“I don’t want to sound like I’m pontificating, but here’s my perspective.”

If you'd like, I can also show **polite ways to disagree in meetings without sounding like you’re pontificating**—very useful for architecture discussions.

# Pound into

**"Pound into"** is a phrasal verb that generally means to **forcefully instill, emphasize, or drive something into someone’s mind or memory** — often through repetition or strong insistence.

It can also be used **literally** for physically pounding something into a surface.

### **🔸 Figurative Meaning (most common):**

To teach or stress something repeatedly until it’s fully understood.

**Examples**:

* *"The coach pounded the importance of teamwork into the players."*

* *"Our teacher pounded the formulas into our heads before the exam."*

### **🔸 Literal Meaning:**

To physically hit or hammer something into another surface.

* *"He pounded the nails into the wood."*

* *"They pounded the stakes into the ground."*

### **🔸 Synonyms (figurative):**

* Drill into

* Hammer into

* Instill in

* Impress upon

# Pull a 180

Pull a 180” is an informal phrase that means to **make a complete reversal or sudden change in direction, opinion, or behavior**.

👉 It comes from the idea of turning **180 degrees** — basically turning around and going the opposite way.

### **Examples:**

* *“He was against the idea at first, but then he pulled a 180 and fully supported it.”*

* *“She was going to quit her job, but then she pulled a 180 and decided to stay.”*

* *“I thought the movie was boring, but halfway through it pulled a 180 and got really exciting.”*

### **Spoken Sentences You Can Use:**

1. “At first, I didn’t like the proposal, but then I kind of pulled a 180.”

2. “The client pulled a 180 during the meeting — suddenly they were all in.”

3. “I was planning to skip the event, but I pulled a 180 and showed up.”

4. “The discussion pulled a 180 once new data came in.”

✅ You can use this in both casual and professional settings, though it’s more common in casual speech.  
 For more formal contexts, you could say **“made a complete turnaround”** or **“completely reversed their position.”**

# Pull Up

1️⃣ **To stop a vehicle**  
 Often used when arriving somewhere and bringing the vehicle to a halt.

**Meaning:** Bring a car or other vehicle to a stop.

**Examples:**

* "The taxi pulled up in front of my house."

* "She pulled up at the gas station."

2️⃣ **To retrieve or display information**  
 Common in workplace or tech contexts.

**Meaning:** Open or bring something into view on a device or system.

* "Let me pull up the report on my laptop."

* "Can you pull up that document from last week?"

3️⃣ **To join or come closer**  
 Used as an invitation in casual situations.

**Meaning:** Move near and become part of the group or activity.

* "Pull up a chair and have dinner with us."

* "Come on, pull up and join the game."

4️⃣ **To confront or correct someone**  
 More common in UK/Australia English.

**Meaning:** Call out or point out someone’s mistake or behaviour.

* "She pulled me up for being late again."

* "He pulled her up about that incorrect figure."

5️⃣ **To improve performance**  
 Usually about grades, standards, or results.

**Meaning:** Raise quality, skill, or achievement.

* "You need to pull up your grades before the finals."

* "He pulled up his performance in the second half."

6️⃣ **To arrive (slang)**  
 Casual/street usage meaning to show up somewhere.

**Meaning:** Come to a place, often unexpectedly or casually.

* "We pulled up to the party at midnight."

* "He just pulled up outside my house."

7️⃣ **To approach aggressively (slang)**  
 Street slang with a confrontational tone.

**Meaning:** Go toward someone to challenge or confront.

* "They pulled up on him in the parking lot."

* "You talking big—pull up then\!"

8️⃣ **Special uses**  
 Different contexts with more specific meanings.

**Meaning:** Varies by field.

* **Fitness:** "I can do 12 pull-ups now."

* **Aviation:** "The pilot had to pull up to avoid the mountain."

* **Gardening:** "We pulled up the weeds in the garden."

### **1️⃣ To stop a vehicle**

When a car or bike **pulls up**, it stops.

**Example:**

* “The taxi pulled up outside my house.”

* “Can you pull up near the entrance?”

👉 This is very common in daily conversation.

### **2️⃣ To improve performance**

It can also mean to **improve or recover from a bad situation**.

* “We need to pull up our sales numbers this quarter.”

* “The team pulled up after a weak start.”

Here it means **to raise or improve something**.

### **3️⃣ To open something on a screen**

In technical or office conversations, it means **to display something**.

* “Let me pull up the dashboard.”

* “Can you pull up the logs from yesterday?”

💡 As a Databricks/MLOps Architect, you can use this often in meetings.

### **4️⃣ Gym context (noun) – “Pull-up”**

A **pull-up** is an exercise where you lift your body by pulling yourself up on a bar.

## **🔹 How you can use it in meetings**

Here are some natural spoken sentences you can practice:

1. “Let me pull up the architecture diagram so we can walk through it.”

2. “Can you pull up the error logs? That might give us more clarity.”

3. “The system performance dipped initially, but it pulled up after optimization.”

4. “Hold on a second — I’ll just pull up the metrics dashboard.”

Notice the connecting phrases:

* *so we can…*

* *that might…*

* *hold on a second…*

* *after that…*

These help your speech flow naturally.

## **🔹 Other contexts where it's used**

* Police stopping someone: “The officer signaled him to pull up.”

* Emotional context (rare but used): “He pulled himself up after the setback.”

* Sports commentary

### **🗣️ Speaking Practice (Say this aloud)**

Try saying:

“Let me quickly pull up the report. After that, we can review the key metrics together.”

# Push back

**“Push back”** is a very useful phrase in professional conversations—especially in technical discussions, architecture reviews, and stakeholder meetings.

Meaning (simple explanation)

**Push back** means:

* to **disagree politely**  
* to **challenge an idea**  
* to **delay something**  
* to **resist pressure or expectations**

The exact meaning depends on the context.

Common usages in workplace conversations

1\. Push back \= politely disagree

Example:

I’d like to push back on this approach because it may not scale well in production.

Here, you are not rejecting directly—you are **raising a concern respectfully**.

2\. Push back \= delay something

Let’s push back the deployment to next week.

Meaning: postpone.

3\. Push back \= resist pressure or unrealistic expectations

We should push back on this timeline—it’s too aggressive.

Meaning: negotiate or challenge the timeline.

How architects and technical leaders use it

In architecture discussions, “push back” sounds professional and balanced. It signals:

* critical thinking  
* ownership  
* technical maturity  
* collaboration (not confrontation)

I want to push back slightly on using a monolithic approach here. A modular design may give us better flexibility in the long run.

Notice the softener **“slightly”**—this makes it sound diplomatic.

5 spoken sentences you can use in meetings

Practice saying these aloud:

1. “I’d like to push back a little on this design decision because it might affect scalability later.”  
2. “Maybe we should push back on the deadline until we validate the dependencies.”  
3. “From an architecture perspective, I’d push back on introducing another service here.”  
4. “That being said, I want to push back slightly on the assumption that this will handle peak traffic.”  
5. “We can push back if the requirement isn’t clearly defined yet.”

Try speaking them slowly and confidently. This phrase is very common in senior-level discussions.

Useful connectors to sound more fluent while pushing back

You can combine these:

* “That being said…”  
* “From my perspective…”  
* “At the same time…”  
* “Having said that…”  
* “On the other hand…”

That being said, I’d like to push back on adding another layer here because it increases latency.

Other contexts where “push back” is used

Besides technical meetings:

**Project planning**

We may need to push back the release.

**Requirement discussions**

I think we should push back on unclear requirements.

**Client conversations**

It’s reasonable to push back if expectations are unrealistic.

**Team coordination**

Let’s push back this task until dependencies are resolved.

# Put off

The phrasal verb **“put off”** is very common in spoken English, and it has two main meanings depending on the context.

### **Meanings of put off**

1. **To postpone / delay**

   * *“Let’s put off the meeting until tomorrow.”*

   * *“They had to put off the trip because of the weather.”*

2. **To discourage / make someone lose interest or feel dislike**

   * *“The bad reviews put me off watching that movie.”*

   * *“His attitude really put her off.”*

### **Common spoken sentences to practice**

1. “Can we put off the call until later today?”

2. “On the other hand, if we keep putting things off, we’ll fall behind.”

3. “For example, the noise outside really put me off while I was studying.”

4. “That being said, sometimes putting things off can give us more time to prepare.”

To delay or postpone.  
We put off the trip due to bad weather.

# Put one's finger on the pulse of

The phrase **“put one’s finger on the pulse of”** means to stay closely aware of what is happening, especially trends, changes, or the general mood of people in a group, company, or society.

Think of it like checking someone’s pulse to see how they’re doing—here, you’re “checking the pulse” of a situation to understand it well.

### **Examples in context**

* A business leader might say: *“We need to put our finger on the pulse of what our customers really want.”*

* In politics: *“The candidate has put her finger on the pulse of public opinion.”*

* In tech: *“Startups survive only if they put their finger on the pulse of the latest trends.”*

### **Phrases you can practice speaking**

1. “We need to put our finger on the pulse of customer expectations.”

2. “He has his finger on the pulse of the latest market trends.”

3. “On the other hand, if we don’t put our finger on the pulse, we might miss key opportunities.”

4. “For example, good managers always keep their finger on the pulse of their teams.”

👉 Try saying these aloud slowly, then more naturally, as if in a meeting.

# Put out/off

Extinguish (light/fire/gas etc)

Kindly put out the fire

1\. **To produce or release something**: It means to create or make something available, like a product, music, or work.  
   \- \***Example**\*: "The band put out a new album." (They released a new album.)  
   \- \***Usage**\*: Used when talking about companies, artists, or people creating something for others to use or enjoy.

2\. \*\***To extinguish or stop something burning**\*\*: It means to put an end to a fire, cigarette, or anything burning.  
   \- \***Example**\*: "He put out his cigarette before entering." (He stopped the cigarette from burning.)  
   \- \***Usage**\*: Common when talking about stopping fires or smoking.

3\. \*\***To inconvenience someone**\*\*: It means to cause trouble or extra work for someone.  
   \- \***Example**\*: "I don’t want to put you out, but can you help me?" (I don’t want to trouble you.)  
   \- \***Usage**\*: Used politely when asking for a favor or worrying about bothering someone.

4\. \*\*(Informal, slang) To agree to something, often romantic or sexual\*\*: This is a casual or sometimes crude way to say someone is willing to engage in a romantic or sexual act. (Be careful with this meaning, as it can sound inappropriate.)  
   \- \*Example\*: "She’s not the type to put out on the first date." (She doesn’t agree to intimacy quickly.)  
   \- \*Usage\*: Used in very informal or gossip-like conversations, often among close friends.

\#\#\# How to Use It:  
\- Pay attention to the situation. For example, saying "put out a fire" is safe and clear, but the slang meaning (romantic/sexual) is risky and depends on who you’re talking to.  
\- It’s a casual phrase, so it’s more common in everyday speech than formal writing.

If you hear "put out," think about the context to understand which meaning fits\!

# Put two and two together

“Put two and two together” \= figure something out by combining obvious clues; draw a (usually correct) conclusion.

### **How to use it**

* **Positive:** *When I saw the suitcase and the passport, I put two and two together: she was traveling.*

* **Negative:** *Don’t put two and two together just yet—we don’t have all the facts.*

* **Past form:** *I put two and two together and realized he’d resigned.*

* **With “wrongly”:** *The media put two and two together and got it wrong.*

### **Nuances**

* Implies the answer is not stated, but it’s **easy to infer**.

* Often about everyday detective work, not deep analysis.

### **Synonyms / near equivalents**

* **figure it out**, **connect the dots**, **draw the obvious conclusion**, **add things up**

### **Quick practice**

1. *Anyone could **put two and two together** and see we’re moving offices.*

2. *She saw the calendar reminder and the flowers, and **put two and two together**.*

3. *Let’s not **put two and two together** too quickly.*

[↑ Back to index](#index)

# Put Up

### **1\. To Tolerate (Put Up With)**

* **Meaning**: To endure or tolerate something unpleasant.  
* **Example**: I don’t know how she puts up with his bad behavior.

### **2\. To Raise or Erect**

* **Meaning**: To build or install something.  
* **Example**: They put up a new fence around the garden.

### **3\. To Offer (Money, Resistance, etc.)**

* **Meaning**: To contribute or provide something.  
* **Example**: He put up $1,000 for the project.  
* **Example**: The team put up a good fight, but they lost the match.

### **4\. To Accommodate Someone**

* **Meaning**: To provide a place to stay.  
* **Example**: My friend put me up for the night when I missed my train.

### **5\. To Display or Post**

* **Meaning**: To attach something in a visible place.  
* **Example**: They put up posters for the concert around the city.

### **6\. To Nominate Someone**

* **Meaning**: To suggest someone for a role or position.  
* **Example**: They put him up for the position of team leader.

### **7\. To Preserve (Food)**

* **Meaning**: To store or can food for future use.  
* **Example**: She put up several jars of homemade jam for the winter.

### **8\. To Increase (Prices, Rents, etc.)**

* **Meaning**: To raise or make something more expensive.  
* **Example**: The landlord put up the rent again this year.

**1️⃣ Put up \= Build or Construct**

It means to build something quickly or temporarily.

For example:

* They **put up** a new building near our office.

* The company **put up** a temporary structure for the event.

👉 In simple words: *to build or install something.*

**2️⃣ Put up \= Stay Somewhere (Usually Temporary)**

It means to stay at someone’s house for a short time.

* I’ll **put you up** for the night.

* My friend **put me up** when I visited Mumbai.

👉 Meaning: *to give someone a place to stay.*

**3️⃣ Put up \= Increase (Price, Rate, etc.)**

It means to raise something like price or rent.

* They **put up** the rent this year.

* The company **put up** the product price.

👉 Meaning: *to increase something.*

**4️⃣ Put up \= Display or Show**

It means to place something where people can see it.

* They **put up** a notice on the board.

* We need to **put up** a banner for the event.

👉 Meaning: *to display publicly.*

**5️⃣ Put up with \= Tolerate (Important Variation)**

This is slightly different.

“Put up with” means to tolerate something annoying.

* I can’t **put up with** this noise anymore.

* She **puts up with** a lot of pressure at work.

👉 Meaning: *to tolerate.*

🔹 Spoken English Practice Sentences

Here are natural phrases you can use in meetings or casual talk:

1. “We might need to **put up** a temporary solution until we finalize the architecture.”

2. “On the other hand, if costs go up, the company may **put up** the prices.”

3. “For example, we can **put up** a dashboard to track real-time metrics.”

4. “That being said, I can’t **put up with** repeated production issues.”

5. “In the meantime, we can **put up** the service on a staging environment.”

👉 Try speaking these aloud slowly and clearly. Focus on stress: **PUT up**, **PUT up with**.

**🔹 Extra Context (Professional Usage – Your Field)**

As an Architect or MLOps Engineer, you can use “put up” like this:

* “We can put up a monitoring dashboard.”

* “Let’s put up a temporary workaround.”

* “They’ve put up stricter security policies.”

* “The cloud provider put up new pricing rules.”

To live  
I put up in delhi.

# Ramp up

**“Ramp up”** means **to increase something gradually or quickly**, such as effort, speed, production, work, or intensity.

You’ll hear it a lot in **workplace, tech, startups, and planning discussions**.

### **Common meanings with examples**

1. **Increase effort or activity**  
* *“We need to ramp up our testing before release.”*  
* (Meaning: do more testing)  
2. **Increase speed or scale**  
* *“The team ramped up development last month.”*  
* (Meaning: development speed increased)  
3. **Prepare and build momentum**  
* *“I’m ramping up for the new project.”*  
* (Meaning: getting ready and increasing involvement)

### **Very common spoken sentences (practice these aloud)**

Use these in meetings or casual work talk 👇

1. **“We need to ramp things up a bit.”**  
2. **“Over the next two weeks, we’ll ramp up production.”**  
3. **“I’m still ramping up, but I’ll be fully productive soon.”**  
4. **“Once the basics are done, we can ramp up the pace.”**  
5. **“That being said, we shouldn’t ramp up too fast.”**

👉 Notice the connectors: *once*, *that being said*, *over the next…*

### **Where you’ll commonly use “ramp up”**

* **Tech / Engineering**: ramp up development, ramp up traffic handling  
* **MLOps / Cloud (your area)**:  
  * *“We’ll ramp up the pipeline after validation.”*  
  * *“Auto-scaling helps ramp up capacity during peak load.”*  
* **Personal productivity**: ramp up preparation, ramp up practice  
* **Business**: ramp up hiring, ramp up marketing

### **Grammar tip (gentle correction)**

Your input **“ramp up”** is perfectly fine 👍  
 In full sentences, it’s usually used as:

* **Verb**: *“We need to ramp up performance.”*  
* **Continuous**: *“I’m ramping up slowly.”*

### **Quick contrast (helps fluency)**

* **Ramp up** → increase  
* **Ramp down** → decrease

Example:

*“We ramped up during the launch, and later ramped down operations.”*

### **Practice aloud (30-second drill 🎙️)**

Say this slowly and clearly:

*“Initially, things were slow. However, once we understood the system, we ramped up our efforts. That being said, we were careful not to rush.”*

# Raspiness

**Raspiness** is a **noun** that describes a **rough, harsh, or scratchy quality in a sound**, most often referring to a **voice**. It comes from the word **raspy**, which suggests a grating or coarse sound (like rubbing sandpaper).

### **🔹 Examples in sentences**

* *The raspiness in her singing gave it a unique charm.*

* *Cold weather often brings raspiness to my throat.*

👉 In **spoken English**, people usually use it when talking about voices:

* *I like the raspiness in his voice—it makes the song more emotional.*

* *After a long day of talking, I noticed some raspiness in my throat.*

* *That old actor’s raspiness makes him sound so intense.*

# Ratchet up

**"Ratchet up"** means to gradually increase something, especially in intensity, pressure, or amount — often in a way that’s hard to reverse.

**Examples:**

* *The company ratcheted up its advertising campaign before the product launch.*

* *Tensions ratcheted up between the two countries.*

* *He ratcheted up the pressure until they agreed.*

**Why “ratchet”?**  
 A **ratchet** is a mechanical device that only turns in one direction — once you move it forward, it can’t slip back. So metaphorically, to “ratchet up” something means you keep pushing it higher without letting it drop back down.

There’s also **"ratchet down"**, which means to decrease something gradually.

# Rectify

Meaning of "Rectify / Rectification"

* **Rectify (verb):** to correct something, to make it right, or to fix an error.

* **Rectification (noun):** the act of correcting, adjusting, or making something right.

Think of it as *"turning something wrong into right."*

Different Contexts Where It’s Used

1. **General / Everyday use**

   * Correcting mistakes, misunderstandings, or errors.

   * Example: *“I sent the wrong file yesterday, but I rectified it by sending the updated one today.”*

   * Here, **rectify** \= *fix a mistake*.

2. **Technical / Engineering**

   * In electrical engineering, *rectification* means **converting AC (alternating current) to DC (direct current)** using devices like rectifiers.

   * Example: *“This circuit uses a diode for rectification.”*

3. **Law / Formal Documents**

   * Rectification means **correcting an error in a contract or legal document** so it reflects what was actually intended.

   * Example: *“The court ordered rectification of the agreement due to a drafting mistake.”*

4. **Medical / Professional**

   * Rectify \= correct a condition, imbalance, or issue.

   * Example: *“The doctor suggested exercises to rectify my posture.”*

5. **Spiritual / Astrological**

   * In astrology, rectification means **adjusting a birth chart** when the exact birth time isn’t known, by analyzing life events.

   * Example: *“He did a rectification of my horoscope to find my accurate ascendant.”*

Spoken Sentences You Can Use

* “I’ll rectify the issue as soon as possible.”

* “Thanks for pointing it out; I’ll make the rectification right away.”

* “On the other hand, if the document isn’t clear, rectification might be needed.”

* “That being said, once it’s rectified, we shouldn’t face the same problem again.”

👉 A tip: *“Rectify” sounds a bit formal*, so in casual talk you’d more often hear *“fix,” “correct,” or “sort out.”* Use “rectify” in professional or technical contexts.

# Remark

The word **“remark”** can be used both as a **noun** and a **verb**.

### **🔹 As a noun → a comment or statement**

* *She made a funny remark during the meeting.*

* *His remark offended some people in the audience.*

* *The teacher’s remark encouraged the student.*

### **🔹 As a verb → to say or comment**

* *He remarked that the weather was unusually hot.*

* *“That’s interesting,” she remarked thoughtfully.*

* *The judge remarked on the importance of honesty.*

👉 In **spoken English**, “remark” is a bit **formal**, so in casual talk, people more often say **“comment,” “say,” “point out,”** or just speak directly.

**Spoken-style examples:**

* *The manager remarked that we need to be more efficient.*

* *I just wanted to remark on how well you did today.*

* *She remarked that the food was excellent.*

# Rest assured

The phrase **“rest assured”** means *“be confident”* or *“don’t worry, you can trust this will happen.”*  
 It’s often used in both formal and casual speech to reassure someone.

Examples in context:

* **Professional:** *“Rest assured, we are taking all necessary steps to resolve this issue.”*

* **Casual:** *“Rest assured, I’ll take care of everything for the trip.”*

* **Customer service:** *“Rest assured, your payment has been processed successfully.”*

Alternative phrases:

* “You can be sure…”

* “Don’t worry…”

* “I can guarantee…”

Practice sentences you can use in real life:

1. *“Rest assured, I’ll update you once the deployment is successful.”*

2. *“Rest assured, your concerns have been noted and we’ll take care of them.”*

3. *“You can rest assured that everything is under control.”*

4. *“Rest assured, I’ll handle the client call if you’re unavailable.”*

👉 Try speaking these aloud slowly, then naturally.

# Rip apart

Meaning of rip apart

1️⃣ Literal meaning

To tear something into pieces physically.

For example:

* The dog **ripped apart** the pillow.  
* The storm **ripped apart** the roof.

👉 Here, it means tearing something into pieces.

2️⃣ Figurative meaning (very common in professional conversations)

It means:

* To strongly criticize something  
* To destroy something emotionally  
* To break something into parts for deep analysis

* The manager **ripped apart** the proposal in the meeting.  
* The critics **ripped the movie apart**.  
* Let’s **rip this design apart** and see what we can improve.

👉 In tech discussions, it often means *reviewing very critically*.

In your professional context (Architecture / MLOps)

You might hear:

* “Let’s rip apart this architecture and rebuild it properly.”  
* “The client ripped apart our cost estimation.”  
* “We should rip apart the current pipeline and optimize it.”

That being said, be careful — it sounds strong and informal. In formal meetings, you might soften it.

Instead of:

“He ripped apart my idea.”

You can say:

“He gave very strong critical feedback.”  
 “He challenged the proposal quite aggressively.”

3–5 Spoken Sentences You Can Practice

Try saying these aloud:

1. “To be honest, the reviewer completely ripped apart my proposal, but I learned a lot from it.”  
2. “Let’s rip apart this design step by step and see where the bottlenecks are.”  
3. “On the other hand, constructive criticism helps us improve, even if it feels like our work is being ripped apart.”  
4. “At first, I felt bad, but later I realized the feedback was useful.”

Practice speaking them slowly and confidently.

Extra Usage Context

It can also mean emotional damage:

* “The breakup really ripped him apart.”  
* “The incident ripped the family apart.”

So depending on context, it can be:

* Physical damage  
* Strong criticism  
* Emotional pain

Now let me challenge you 😊

Explain in your own words:  
 👉 *“When would you use ‘rip apart’ in a technical design review?”*

# Rule of Thumb

A **rule of thumb** is a **general principle, rough guideline, or practical way of doing something** based on experience rather than strict rules or scientific accuracy.

It’s not exact, but it works well enough in everyday situations.

### **Usage in sentences**

* *As a rule of thumb, you should drink about 2 liters of water a day.*

* *A good rule of thumb for cooking pasta is 1 liter of water for every 100 grams of pasta.*

* *As a rule of thumb, investments with higher returns usually carry higher risks.*

### **In spoken English**

You’ll often hear it when someone gives **practical advice or a quick estimate**:

* *“How long should I let the paint dry?”*  
   → *“Well, as a rule of thumb, give it 24 hours.”*

* *“How much should I tip here?”*  
   → *“Rule of thumb is about 10–15%.”*

✅ **Key idea:** A *rule of thumb* is not precise but is **useful as a shortcut guideline**.

[↑ Back to index](#index)

# Run into Rough weather

The phrase "run into rough weather" is an idiom that means encountering difficulties, challenges, or problems, especially unexpectedly. It can be used literally, such as a ship encountering bad weather at sea, or metaphorically, such as facing struggles in life, work, or relationships.

Examples:  
Literal:  
"The sailors ran into rough weather while crossing the Atlantic."  
(The ship faced bad weather.)

Metaphorical:

"The project ran into rough weather when the client suddenly changed their requirements."  
(The project faced unexpected difficulties.)  
"Their marriage ran into rough weather after they moved to a new city."  
(Their marriage encountered problems or challenges.)

# Run past

The phrase **“run past”** is usually used in two ways:

1. **To physically run by someone/something**  
    → Example: *“I saw him run past me on the street.”*  
    (Here, it means someone literally ran by you.)

2. **To check/confirm something with someone** (common in office talk)  
    → Example: *“Let me run this past my manager before we finalize it.”*  
    (Here, it means “let me discuss or confirm this with someone.”)

### **Common Sentences You Can Use**

* *“Let me run this past you and see if it makes sense.”*

* *“I’ll run it past the team before we send it to the client.”*

* *“Can I run a quick idea past you?”*

* *“I’ll run past the grocery store on my way home.”* (casual, physical meaning)

### **Context of Usage**

* **Workplace**: When you want to confirm something with your manager, team, or client.

* **Casual conversation**: When you want to quickly check something with a friend.

* **Literal**: When talking about running/jogging and passing someone or something.

👉 Try saying these aloud to get the flow. For example:  
 *“Before I make changes, I’ll run this past my manager.”*

[↑ Back to index](#index)

# Run past someone

When you *“run something past someone”*, it means you **quickly explain an idea, plan, or detail to them to get their opinion or approval**.  
 It’s informal, often used in workplace or casual conversations.

For example:

* *“Let me run this idea past my manager before we finalize it.”*

* *“Can I run the draft past you to see if it makes sense?”*

So in your case, *“run past some details someone”* probably means: *“share or explain some details to someone for their feedback/approval.”*

### **Common Spoken Phrases You Can Use**

1. “Let me just run this past you to make sure we’re aligned.”

2. “I’ll run the numbers past the finance team before confirming.”

3. “Can I run a quick thought past you?”

4. “Before we proceed, I’d like to run this past my lead.”

### **Usage Contexts**

* **Work meetings**: when you want to check with a colleague/manager.

* **Project discussions**: before sending something out.

* **Casual talk**: even with friends, like running past them a vacation plan.

# Saber-rattling

**"Saber-rattling"** means threatening or showing aggression — especially in a political or military context — to intimidate or warn others, often without actually taking action.

**Examples:**

* *The country’s recent missile tests are seen as saber-rattling.*

* *His speech was full of saber-rattling but no real plan for war.*

**Origin:**  
 It comes from the image of soldiers shaking or rattling their **sabers** (swords) to show they’re ready to fight — more about intimidation than actual combat.

It’s almost always used in a **figurative sense** today, usually for governments, leaders, or powerful groups making threats.

# Scapegoat

A **scapegoat** is a person who is unfairly **blamed for something** that was not really their fault.

* Example: *“When the project failed, the manager made me the scapegoat.”*

* Example: *“Politicians sometimes use others as scapegoats to cover their own mistakes.”*

👉 It comes from an old tradition where a goat was symbolically burdened with people’s sins and sent away. That’s why today it means blaming someone unfairly.

✅ **Spoken practice sentences:**

* “He became the scapegoat for the company’s failures.”

* “On the other hand, sometimes leaders need to take responsibility instead of finding a scapegoat.”

* “That being said, people often look for a scapegoat when things go wrong.”

* “I don’t want to be the scapegoat for someone else’s mistake.”

👉 **Context of usage:**

* **Workplace:** *“They blamed the intern—it felt like he was made the scapegoat.”*

* **Politics:** *“The opposition is using him as a scapegoat for the crisis.”*

* **Casual talk:** *“Don’t make me the scapegoat for this\!”* (light or joking way).

# Scratch your own itch

To do something out of motivation to solve a personal problem

👉 *Solve a problem that you personally face, rather than trying to guess what problems others have.*

It’s often used in business, startups, programming, and self-improvement. The idea is: if something bothers you, fix it — and that solution may help others too.

### **Meaning in simple terms**

* If you have an itch, scratching it relieves you.

* Similarly, if you face a problem, creating a solution helps you (and maybe others who have the same problem).

### **Usage examples**

1. **In startups / business**

   * *“Many successful products are built when founders scratch their own itch. They face a problem, solve it for themselves, and later realize millions of others need the same solution.”*

2. **In programming**

   * *“I built this tool to automate my daily reports. It was just me scratching my own itch, but later my team started using it too.”*

3. **In personal life**

   * *“Instead of waiting for someone to create the kind of workout planner I wanted, I made my own. Just scratching my own itch.”*

✅ So, whenever you hear *“scratch your own itch”*, think: **solve your own problem first — that’s often the best way to create something useful.**

# Self fulfilling prophecy

A **self-fulfilling prophecy** is when a belief or expectation (about yourself, others, or a situation) actually **causes itself to become true**—because the belief influences actions and behavior in a way that makes it happen.

✅ **Examples:**

* *If a teacher believes a student is very bright, they may give extra attention and encouragement, which helps the student perform better—fulfilling the prophecy.*

* *If you keep telling yourself, “I’ll fail this exam,” you might lose confidence, study less, and actually fail—proving your own negative belief true.*

* *Believing you’ll succeed in a job interview can boost your confidence, which makes you perform better—again, a self-fulfilling prophecy.*

👉 In **spoken English**, people often use it casually like:

* *I didn’t want to believe it, but it turned into a self-fulfilling prophecy.*

* *Be careful—thinking negatively can be a self-fulfilling prophecy.*

[↑ Back to index](#index)

# Shard of Glass

Literal meaning:

Metaphorical uses and expressions:

1. Emotional Pain/Heartbreak "Her words felt like shards of glass in my heart" Think of when someone breaks up with you, and every memory feels sharp and painful.  
2. Harsh/Cutting Speech "His bitter comments were like shards of glass in their conversation" Similar to when someone's words are intentionally hurtful and cutting.  
3. In Poetry/Literature (for beauty) "The winter morning sun sparkled on the icicles like shards of glass hanging from the roof" Used to describe beautiful, crystalline things.  
4. Mental State "Her mind felt like shards of glass \- fragmented and sharp" Describing scattered, painful thoughts or broken concentration.

A very memorable example: Imagine dropping your mother's favorite crystal vase. You see it fall in slow motion, and when it hits the floor, it breaks into shards. Each piece catches light differently \- beautiful but dangerous to touch. This image works perfectly to describe any situation that's both delicate and potentially harmful, whether it's a fragile relationship, a difficult conversation, or a complex emotional situation.

Shards can also be made of pottery or metal.  
A shard can also refer to a small piece or part of something. For example, "little shards of time and space recorded by the camera's lens".   
In movies, "shard of glass" can also refer to a horrible truth, a blind spot, or something buried in the hero. For example, in Alien, the company Sigourney Weaver believes in considers her and the crew of Nostromo expendable.

# Size out

The phrase **“size out”** can mean different things depending on context:

1. **Clothing / Fitting context**

   * If someone *“sizes out”* of clothing, it means they no longer fit into that size — either they’ve grown bigger (outgrown it) or, in some cases, become smaller.

   * Example: *“He sized out of kids’ clothes last year.”*

2. **Manufacturing / Production context**

   * It can mean to measure or cut something according to a required size.

   * Example: *“We need to size out the metal sheets before assembly.”*

3. **Business / Planning context**

   * Sometimes used informally to mean *estimating or scoping the size of something*.

   * Example: *“Let’s size out the project before committing resources.”*

✅ **Sentences you can practice speaking**:

* “I sized out of my old jeans, so I had to buy a new pair.”

* “Before we start, we need to size out the scope of this task.”

* “The kids usually size out of their shoes pretty quickly.”

* “On the other hand, sometimes people size out when they lose weight too.”

👉 Try saying them aloud, and notice how naturally *“size out”* blends in with casual or professional talk.

# Slip in

Slip in” is a **phrasal verb** that usually means to **enter, insert, or say something quietly, casually, or without much notice**.  
 Its meaning depends on the context:

1. **Enter quietly or secretly**

   * *He slipped in through the back door.*  
      (He entered quietly without drawing attention.)

2. **Add something casually**

   * *She slipped in a joke during the meeting.*  
      (She added it naturally without making it obvious.)

3. **Insert something into a space**

   * *I slipped the letter into his bag.*  
      (I put it there quietly or discreetly.)

✅ **Spoken examples you could practice**

* “Sorry I’m late, I just slipped in at the back.”

* “If you don’t mind, I’d like to slip in a quick question.”

* “She managed to slip in a few comments without anyone noticing.”

* “On the other hand, you could slip in an extra detail to make your point stronger.”

💡 **Where you might use it:**

* **Meetings**: “Can I just slip in a quick point here?”

* **Casual talk**: “I slipped in while the movie had already started.”

* **Presentations**: “Let me slip in an example to clarify this.”

👉 Try saying this aloud:  
 “Excuse me, may I slip in a quick question before we move on?”

# Something or other

“**Something or other**” is a casual phrase used when you don’t remember exactly what it was, or when the detail isn’t important. It’s a way of being vague on purpose.

👉 For example:

* *“He was talking about some new technology—AI something or other.”*

* *“She asked me to pick up sugar, salt, and something or other from the shop. I forgot the last one.”*

* *“We’ll discuss security, cost, and something or other in tomorrow’s meeting.”*

It’s like saying “some unspecified thing” or “whatever it was.”

✅ Phrases you can practice saying in conversations:

1. *“He mentioned something or other, but I didn’t catch the full detail.”*

2. *“We need to fix the database, the pipeline, and something or other with the permissions.”*

3. *“She’s working on a finance project—tax something or other, I think.”*

4. *“They were arguing about some policy or other; I didn’t pay attention.”*

👉 You can use it in casual speech, storytelling, or when you don’t want to sound too precise but still keep the conversation flowing.

### **Scenario: Office Meeting**

Suppose you’re telling a colleague about a discussion you overheard:

👤 You: *“The client was worried about costs, compliance, and something or other with security. I didn’t catch the last part properly.”*

👉 Here, you use **“something or other”** because you don’t remember the exact third issue, but you want to keep the flow of conversation.

### **Scenario: Casual Conversation**

You’re talking to a friend about a movie:

👤 You: *“It was a superhero movie—Spider-Man something or other. I don’t remember the exact title.”*

👉 Here, you’re being vague, but the listener still understands you mean *some Spider-Man movie*.

### **Scenario: Explaining to a Team**

During a project update:

👤 You: *“We need to fix the ETL pipeline, the data schema, and something or other with the reporting layer. I’ll confirm the details later.”*

👉 Here, it shows you don’t recall the exact third item but still keep your explanation moving.

✅ Practice Sentences:

1. *“He bought chips, soda, and something or other from the shop.”*

2. *“She said she’ll join a training—cloud something or other.”*

3. *“They were discussing budgets, deadlines, and something or other.”*

[↑ Back to index](#index)

# Sound like a broken record

The phrase **“sound like a broken record”** means **to repeat the same thing over and over**, usually to the point of being annoying or boring.

It comes from the days of vinyl records — when a record was scratched, the needle would get stuck and repeat the same line endlessly. 🎶

✅ **Examples in sentences:**

* *I sound like a broken record, but please finish your homework on time.*

* *He keeps reminding us about the budget cuts—he’s starting to sound like a broken record.*

* *I don’t want to sound like a broken record, but this problem really needs fixing.*

👉 In conversation, it’s often used when you feel you’ve said something many times but no one is listening or acting on it.

[↑ Back to index](#index)

# Space out

The phrase **“space out”** has two common meanings:

1. **Lose focus / daydream** (informal, very common in speech)

   * *Sorry, I spaced out during the meeting. What were you saying?*

   * *He often spaces out in class when the lecture gets boring.*

2. **Place things at intervals / give space**

   * *The chairs are spaced out evenly across the hall.*

   * *Let’s space out the deadlines so we don’t feel rushed.*

👉 In everyday spoken English, people usually mean the **first one** (losing focus, daydreaming).

**Literal meaning:** To lose focus or become mentally distant.  
 **Figurative meaning:** To daydream or momentarily “disconnect.”

🗣 *“Sorry, I spaced out — what were you saying?”*  
 🗣 *“He just spaces out during long meetings.”*

💡 *(Casual, friendly tone — used when someone loses attention briefly.)*

# Spillover

### **Literal meaning**

When something physically flows over the edge because there’s too much.

* *“The tea caused a **spillover** on the table.”*

* *“The dam couldn’t hold the water, so there was a spillover.”*

### **Figurative meaning (much more common in spoken/work settings)**

It means **an effect or result that spreads into another area, often unintentionally.**

Examples in spoken English:

* **Work-life balance:**  
   *“Sometimes stress at work has a **spillover** into family life.”*

* **Economics/Business:**  
   *“The slowdown in China created a **spillover effect** on global markets.”*

* **General situations:**  
   *“The excitement from the festival had a positive **spillover** into the nearby towns.”*  
   *“Arguments at home can cause a **spillover** at school for kids.”*

✅ **In everyday speech**, “spillover” is often paired with **“effect”** → *“spillover effect.”*  
 It sounds a bit formal, but it’s common in discussions about work, economics, psychology, or social life.

The phrase **“spill over”** means something that **extends beyond its normal limits** — like when water spills over the edge of a glass. In spoken English, we often use it metaphorically to describe when **one thing starts affecting another area**.

### **💡 Common meanings and examples:**

1. **To overflow or go beyond boundaries (literal)**

   * “The coffee **spilled over** the cup.”  
      *(It literally went over the edge.)*

2. **To extend into another area or situation (figurative)**

   * “The meeting **spilled over** into lunch time.”  
      *(It continued longer than planned.)*

   * “His frustration at work **spilled over** into his personal life.”  
      *(His emotions carried into another part of his life.)*

3. **To have consequences beyond the original situation**

   * “The conflict in one region **spilled over** into neighboring countries.”  
      *(The effects spread to nearby areas.)*

### **🗣️ Useful spoken phrases**

* “Sorry, our discussion kind of **spilled over** into the next meeting slot.”

* “I didn’t mean to let my stress **spill over** into our conversation.”

* “The workload from last week **spilled over** into this week.”

* “The excitement **spilled over** into the entire team.”

* “We need to manage this before it **spills over** to other projects.”

### **🔗 Connecting phrases you can use with it:**

* “As a result, it **spilled over** into…”

* “On the other hand, we can’t let this issue **spill over** into production.”

* “For example, if testing is delayed, it might **spill over** to deployment.”

[↑ Back to index](#index)

# Spot

1. **Noun – a place or location**

   * *“This is a nice spot for lunch.”* (place)

   * *“He found a quiet spot to read.”*

2. **Noun – a mark or stain**

   * *“There’s a spot on your shirt.”*

3. **Verb – to notice or recognize**

   * *“I spotted him in the crowd.”* (noticed/recognized)

   * *“Can you spot the difference between these two pictures?”*

4. **Noun – a short amount of time / opportunity**

   * *“She got a spot on the TV show.”* (opportunity, position)

   * *“Can I have a spot of your time?”* (a little time – more British usage)

5. **Noun – a minor role or turn**

   * *“He did a comedy spot at the event.”* (performance slot)

1. **“On the spot”** → immediately

   * *“They hired me on the spot.”*

   * *“He had to answer on the spot.”*

2. **“Put someone on the spot”** → make them feel pressured or awkward

   * *“I don’t want to put you on the spot, but can you explain this now?”*

3. **“Sweet spot”** → the perfect position or best point

   * *“We finally hit the sweet spot in our pricing.”*

1. **“I spotted a mistake in the report.”**

2. **“We found a great spot for our weekend trip.”**

3. **“She answered the question on the spot.”**

4. **“For example, during meetings, managers sometimes put people on the spot with sudden questions.”**

5. **“That being said, every team has its sweet spot where things just click.”**

# Spot on

* **Spot on** means *exactly right*, *precisely correct*, or *perfectly accurate*.

* It’s informal and mostly used in **spoken English** (especially in British English, but also common elsewhere).

**Usage in communication**

1. **When someone is correct:**

   * *“Your analysis of the problem was spot on.”*  
      (→ means: you were exactly correct.)

2. **When giving feedback:**

   * *“That’s spot on, keep it up\!”*  
      (→ encouragement, telling someone they did it perfectly.)

3. **When agreeing strongly:**

   * *“Yes, that’s spot on—I couldn’t have said it better myself.”*

4. **When describing a solution or action:**

   * *“Her suggestion was spot on for solving the client’s issue.”*

**Common spoken sentences you can practice:**

* “That’s spot on, thanks for pointing it out.”

* “Your explanation was spot on, really clear and precise.”

* “I think your estimate is spot on—we should go with it.”

* “You’re spot on about the risks we might face.”

👉 **Context of usage**:  
 You’ll often hear “spot on” in **meetings, feedback discussions, casual conversations, or even presentations** when acknowledging correctness. It adds a natural, conversational tone.

💡 Try practicing aloud:

* Say: *“That’s spot on, exactly what I was thinking.”*

Meaning of “bang on”

* Similar to **“spot on”** → it means *exactly right* or *completely correct*.

* It’s **slightly more informal** than *spot on* and more common in **British English**.

* In American English, you might not hear “bang on” as often; people usually say *“right on”* or *“exactly right”*.

1. **Agreement / correctness**

   * *“You’re bang on about the market trend.”*  
      (→ you’re completely correct.)

2. **Describing accuracy**

   * *“His timing was bang on.”*  
      (→ he was perfectly on time.)

3. **In casual conversations**

   * *“That guess was bang on\!”*  
      (→ the guess was exactly right.)

Common spoken sentences to practice

* “You’re bang on with that suggestion.”

* “Her prediction was bang on, things happened exactly as she said.”

* “Your assessment is bang on—we should follow it.”

👉 **Spot on vs. Bang on**

* *Spot on* → can be used in both **formal and informal** contexts.

* *Bang on* → usually **informal**, more casual, works best in friendly conversations or relaxed meetings.

💡Practice aloud:

* *“You’re bang on about the client’s concerns—we need to address them.”*

### **Meaning of “spot on”**
