# Sprinkle them in

👉 **Meaning:**  
 “Sprinkle them in” is an informal phrase that means to add something here and there in small amounts, usually in a subtle way.

* Example: *“When you’re writing your report, sprinkle in a few real-life examples to make it more engaging.”*

* Example: *“The chef sprinkled in some herbs to bring out the flavor.”*

So in conversation, it means: **to include a little bit of something naturally, without overdoing it.**

✅ **Phrases you can practice saying aloud:**

1. *“I try to sprinkle in some humor during meetings to keep the mood light.”*

2. *“You can sprinkle in a few connecting phrases to make your speech flow better.”*

3. *“On the other hand, if you sprinkle in too many details, the audience might get lost.”*

4. *“That being said, it’s always good to sprinkle in some appreciation for your team.”*

💡 **Context of usage:**

* At work: Adding small ideas, tips, or examples in discussions.

* In writing: Adding variety, like examples or jokes.

* In cooking: Literally sprinkling ingredients.

# Stalk

**Verb**

* **To follow or pursue stealthily** (animal/hunter): *The tiger stalked its prey.*

* **To follow someone obsessively or harass them** (criminal/online): *He was arrested for stalking his ex.*

* **To walk away angrily or with purpose** (phrasal: *stalk off / stalk out*): *She stalked out of the meeting.*

**Noun**

* **The main stem of a plant** (or petiole): *The celery stalk.*

* **A period or instance of following/harassing someone**: *There were several stalks reported.* (less common)

**Pronunciation**

* Roughly: **stawk** — IPA: **/stɔːk/** (similar in US/UK). One-syllable, final /k/ pronounced.

**Usage notes**

* When used about people, **“stalk” has a strongly negative, often criminal sense** (real-world/online stalking). Use carefully.

* As a plant-term it’s neutral and common in everyday English.

* “Stalk off” / “stalk out” \= leave angrily; informal but common.

**Examples**

* Animal/hunting: *The cougar stalked the deer for hours before pouncing.*

* Harassment: *She changed her number after discovering someone was stalking her online.*

* Angry exit: *After the argument he stalked out of the room.*

* Plant: *Trim the outer stalks before cooking.*

**Collocations (common pairs)**

* **stalk someone / stalk someone online / stalk an ex**

* **stalk prey**

* **stalk off / stalk out**

* **celery stalk / flower stalk / stalk of a plant**

**Synonyms & antonyms**

* Synonyms (follow/harass sense): **trail, shadow, pursue, harass**

* Antonyms (leave angrily sense): **linger, remain** (not exact opposites)

* For plant sense: **stem, petiole**

**Warning / safety note**

If **you or someone is being stalked**, take it seriously: save evidence (messages/screenshots), block the person, tell trusted people, and contact local authorities or support services.

**Quick practice (say aloud)**

1. “The fox stalked the henhouse at night.”

2. “He kept calling and following her — that’s stalking.”

3. “Fed up with the meeting, she stalked off.”

[↑ Back to index](#index)

# Stand out

The phrase **“stand out”** means to be **noticeable, different, or better** than others in some way. 🌟

### **🔹 Common Uses**

1. **To be easily noticed (appearance or behavior):**

* *Her red dress made her stand out in the crowd.*

* *The tall building stands out against the skyline.*

2. **To be exceptional or remarkable (skills/qualities):**

* *She stands out as one of the best players on the team.*

👉 In **spoken English**, it’s often used as praise or to highlight uniqueness:

* *Wow, that idea really stands out\!*

* *If you want to get hired, your resume needs to stand out.*

* *He stood out because of his confidence and clear communication.*

# Start off (start (something) off)

**"Start off"** means to **begin something**, especially when talking about the **initial phase** of an activity, journey, conversation, or process.

It is a **phrasal verb** and is very common in **spoken English**.

**🔹 Usage in Spoken English:**

Used casually and professionally to refer to how something **begins**.

**✅ Spoken Examples:**

1. **"Let’s start off with a quick introduction."**  
    → *(Let’s begin by introducing ourselves.)*

2. **"He started off as a junior developer and now he’s the CTO."**  
    → *(His career began at a lower level.)*

3. **"The movie started off slow but got really interesting."**

4. **"I want to start off by thanking everyone for coming."**  
    → *(Common in speeches or meetings.)*

🔹 Similar verbs:

* Start

* Begin

* Kick off (informal, often used for events)

**🔹 Difference between "start" and "start off":**

* **"Start"** is general.

* **"Start off"** often emphasizes the **initial stage** or **manner** of beginning.

  * 🔸 *"Let’s start the meeting."* (neutral)

  * 🔸 *"Let’s start off by reviewing last week’s notes."* (more detailed, structured start)  
  * 

to begin by doing something, or to make something begin by doing something:

She started off the meeting with the monthly sales report.

I'd like to start off by thanking you all for coming today.

 Fewer examples

I'm going to start off by introducing two new members of staff.

He started off by summarizing Martin's sales report.

He started off as a children's entertainer.

I started off my career in a small company in Birmingham.

I started off the day well enough but I'm beginning to droop.

“Start off” means **to begin something**, especially in an informal, spoken way.

It is very common in conversations, meetings, and presentations.

### **1️⃣ To begin something**

* “Let’s start off the meeting.”

* “I’ll start off with a quick introduction.”

* “The day started off well.”

Here, “start” and “start off” are similar, but **“start off” sounds more natural in casual speech**.

## **2️⃣ To describe how something begins (tone or situation)**

You can use it to talk about the initial condition.

* “The project started off smoothly, but later we faced issues.”

* “Our conversation started off a bit awkward.”

Notice the structure:

“It started off \+ adjective”

For example:

* “It started off great.”

* “It started off badly.”

## **Difference between “start” and “start off”**

Both are correct.  
 However:

* **“Start”** \= neutral, slightly more formal

* **“Start off”** \= more conversational and natural

In spoken English, especially in meetings, “start off” sounds more relaxed.

## **Sentences you can use in real life (Practice these aloud 🎤)**

1. “Let me start off by giving a quick overview of the architecture.”

2. “We started off strong, but then the performance dropped.”

3. “To start off with, I’d like to explain the problem statement.”

4. “The deployment started off smoothly; however, we noticed some latency later.”

5. “I’ll start off, and then we can open it up for discussion.”

Notice the connectors:

* **To start off with…**

* **However…**

* **On the other hand…**

* **That being said…**

## **Other contexts where you can use it**

### **🔹 In presentations**

“Let me start off with the business requirement.”

### **🔹 In storytelling**

“It started off as a small idea, but eventually it became a full product.”

### **🔹 In casual talk**

“The trip started off really well.”

Now, I want you to practice:

👉 Say this aloud:

“Let me start off by explaining the current system design.”

# Sticking point

A **“sticking point”** means a problem, obstacle, or issue that prevents progress in a discussion, negotiation, or process.

For example:

* In business meetings, people often say, *“The main sticking point is the budget.”*

* It can also be used more casually, like *“The sticking point in our travel plans is the visa.”*

👉 Think of it as the “roadblock” that stops things from moving forward smoothly.

### **Phrases you can practice:**

1. *“The sticking point here seems to be the timeline.”*

2. *“One of the major sticking points in this project is resource allocation.”*

3. *“That being said, if we resolve this sticking point, we can move ahead quickly.”*

4. *“On the other hand, the technical issues are less of a sticking point compared to the budget.”*

✨ Tip: You can use this phrase both in **formal settings** (business negotiations, project updates) and in **casual settings** (plans with friends, family discussions).

# Strew across

The phrase **“strew across”** (or **“strewn across”** in past form) means **to scatter or spread things untidily over an area**. It usually creates an image of something being messy or spread everywhere.

### **Usage in sentences**

1. *“Clothes were strewn across the floor.”*

2. *“Papers were strewn across his desk after the meeting.”*

3. *“After the storm, branches were strewn across the road.”*

It’s more common in written or descriptive English, but you’ll also hear it in spoken English, especially when describing a scene.

### **Phrases you can practice**

1. “Books were strewn across the table after the study session.”

2. “On the other hand, if things are organized, they won’t be strewn across the room.”

3. “For example, toys are usually strewn across the floor when kids are playing.”

4. “That being said, the living room looked lively, even though everything was strewn across it.”

# Stumble upon

"Stumble upon" is an idiom that means to discover or learn about something unexpectedly. For example, "I stumbled across this book by chance". 

to discover something by chance, or to meet someone by chance:

"Stumble upon" is a phrasal verb that means to discover or come across something unexpectedly or by chance. It implies finding or encountering something without actively seeking or intending to find it.

Example sentences:

"While hiking in the woods, we stumbled upon a hidden waterfall."  
"I was browsing the internet when I stumbled upon an interesting article about space exploration."  
"She stumbled upon an old book in the attic that turned out to be a valuable first edition."  
In these examples, "stumble upon" conveys the idea of a fortuitous or unplanned discovery. It is often used to describe finding something surprising or valuable by accident.

"Stumble upon" means to discover or find something or someone unexpectedly or by chance.   
Here's a more detailed explanation:

Meaning: The phrase "stumble upon" implies an accidental or unplanned encounter or discovery.  

Synonyms: You could also say "chance upon," "come across," or "find by accident". 

Examples:  
"I stumbled upon an old photo album while cleaning out the attic".   
"The researchers stumbled upon a new species of insect during their expedition".   
"They stumbled upon a hidden treasure while digging in their backyard".

# Table

The verb "table" has multiple meanings, including to put something aside, to submit something for consideration, or to enter something into a list.   
To put something aside   
To set something aside indefinitely  
To postpone or suspend consideration of something  
To leave something for discussion at a later time  
To submit something for consideration   
To present something formally for discussion  
To submit a bill for consideration by a legislative body

To Postpone or Delay (Verb – Common in American English):  
To table something means to set it aside for discussion or action at a later time.  
Example: "The committee decided to table the proposal until next month."

To Present or Submit for Discussion (Verb – Common in British English):  
To table something means to formally bring it up for discussion or consideration.  
Example: "The new policy was tabled in the meeting."

A Systematic Arrangement of Data (Noun – Contextual Use):  
A table can refer to an organized display of information, often in rows and columns.  
Example: "The results were summarized in a table."

Mathematical Context:  
A table can mean a set of values or a computational tool, like multiplication tables or truth tables.

# taint

/teɪnt/  
verb  
past tense: tainted; past participle: tainted

contaminate or pollute (something).  
"the air was tainted by fumes from the cars"

affect with a bad or undesirable quality.  
"his reputation was tainted by scandal"

archaic  
(of food or water) become contaminated or polluted.  
"the rennet should be soaked in water containing sufficient salt to keep it from tainting"

The term "taint" is vulgar slang for the perineum, which is the area between the anus and the genitals.   
"Taint" can also be used as a verb or a noun to describe something that harms the reputation of a person or thing:   
Verb  
When a person or thing is tainted by something bad, their reputation is harmed. For example, "Opposition leaders said that the elections had been tainted by corruption".  
Noun  
An undesirable quality that spoils the reputation of someone or something. For example, "Her government never really shook off the taint of corruption".  
Synonyms of "taint" include disgrace, shame, dishonor, and stigma.   
The term "taint" has been used in comedic shows, including Saturday Night Live and The Daily Show.

# Take away

### **Literal meaning**

1. **To remove something**

   * *The waiter took away the empty plates.*

   * *Please take away your shoes before entering the room.*

2. **Food context (British English)** → “takeaway” (noun) means food you order and eat elsewhere.

   * *We had Chinese takeaway last night.*

### **Figurative / idiomatic meanings**

1. **Main point or lesson learned**

   * *The big takeaway from the meeting is that we need to cut costs.*

   * *What’s your takeaway from that book?*  
      → Here, *takeaway* (noun) \= **the most important thing you learned or remembered**.

2. **To deprive or remove abstractly**

   * *Don’t let anyone take away your confidence.*

   * *Nothing can take away the memories we shared.*

3. **In sports (esp. American football)**

   * A “takeaway” is when the defending team gains possession from the opponent.

   * *The defense had three takeaways in the game.*

### **Spoken examples**

* *“What’s your takeaway from today’s class?”* → What did you learn?

* *“The only takeaway is that we must prepare better next time.”* → The main lesson.

* *“She turned her pain into strength — no one can take that away from her.”*

✅ **Quick note:**

* **Take away (verb)** \= remove.

* **Takeaway (noun)** \= main point OR food to-go.

# Take hold of

### **Meaning of *Take hold of***

It means **to grab, control, or gain possession of something**, either physically or metaphorically.

1. **Physical sense (literally grabbing):**

   * *“He took hold of her hand.”*

   * *“She took hold of the rope and climbed up.”*

2. **Metaphorical sense (when something starts spreading or becoming strong):**

   * *“A sense of fear took hold of him.”*

   * *“The new idea is starting to take hold in the company.”*

   * *“Once the habit takes hold of you, it’s hard to break.”*

✅ **Spoken practice sentences:**

* “She took hold of my arm to stop me.”

* “On the other hand, fear can easily take hold of us if we’re not careful.”

* “That being said, positive habits can also take hold of our daily lives.”

* “The new technology has really taken hold in the market.”

👉 **Contexts of usage:**

* **Daily talk:** *“He took hold of the steering wheel.”*

* **Work/professional:** *“This trend is beginning to take hold in the industry.”*

* **Emotional/abstract:** *“Excitement took hold of the crowd.”*

✨ Small tip: In casual speech, people often replace it with simpler verbs like **“grab,” “catch,” “control,” or “spread”** depending on the context.

# Take over

The phrase **"take over"** is commonly used to describe the act of gaining control, dominance, or responsibility over something or someone. It has various applications depending on the context. Here's a breakdown of how it's generally used:  
---

### **1\. Leadership or Authority**

**"Take over"** often refers to assuming control or responsibility for something, such as a role, task, or organization.

* Example: "She will take over as the new manager next month."  
  * Meaning: She will assume the responsibilities of the manager.

### **2\. Business or Ownership**

In a business context, **"take over"** refers to acquiring control of a company or organization, often through purchase or merger.

* Example: "The larger corporation took over the smaller one."  
  * Meaning: The larger company gained ownership and control of the smaller company.

### **3\. Power or Control**

It can mean gaining dominance over a situation, place, or group.

* Example: "The rebels took over the city."  
  * Meaning: The rebels seized control of the city.

### **4\. Emotions or Thoughts**

It can describe when emotions, feelings, or thoughts dominate someone's mind or actions.

* Example: "Anger took over, and he couldn't think rationally."  
  * Meaning: Anger became so strong that it controlled his behavior.

### **5\. Technology or Automation**

Refers to machines, systems, or processes taking control or replacing human involvement.

* Example: "Robots are taking over many manual jobs."  
  * Meaning: Robots are replacing humans in certain roles.

### **6\. Physical Spread**

Can describe something spreading and dominating an area.

* Example: "The weeds have taken over the garden."  
  * Meaning: Weeds have spread so much that they dominate the garden.

### **General Purpose**

"Take over" is versatile and usually implies **control, responsibility, or dominance** in different contexts. It often suggests that the original person, system, or thing in control has been replaced.

"It takes you over" means something becomes so strong or overwhelming that it controls or dominates you. It often refers to emotions, thoughts, or situations that you can't resist or escape. The phrase suggests being consumed or overtaken by something.

### **Examples:**

1. **Emotionally:**  
   * "When fear takes you over, it’s hard to think clearly."  
   * Meaning: Fear becomes so intense that it controls your actions or thoughts.  
2. **Physically:**  
   * "The exhaustion takes you over after a long day."  
   * Meaning: Exhaustion becomes so overwhelming that you can’t fight it anymore.  
3. **Metaphorically:**  
   * "The music takes you over, and you can't help but dance."  
   * Meaning: The music influences you so strongly that it controls your behavior.

It’s often used to describe a powerful, consuming experience—positive or negative\!

Meaning: To assume control or responsibility for something.

* Example 1: The company plans to take over its competitor’s market share.  
* Example 2: When the manager resigned, his assistant had to take over the responsibilities.

**Meaning:** To assume control or responsibility.  
 **Figurative meaning:** To dominate or replace something.

🗣 *“AI is taking over routine tasks.”*  
 🗣 *“He took over as team leader last month.”*

**Take over** – To assume control or responsibility.  
 She took over the project after John left.

# Take someone’s word for it

“Take someone’s word for it” means to **believe or trust what someone says without needing proof, evidence, or further explanation**.

It’s often used when you decide not to verify something yourself, but instead rely on what the other person is saying.

### **Examples:**

* *“I didn’t check the numbers myself; I just took his word for it.”*

* *“You don’t have to show me the details, I’ll take your word for it.”*

* *“She says the project is on track — I’ll take her word for it.”*

### **Spoken Sentences You Can Use:**

1. “If you say the system is stable, I’ll take your word for it.”

2. “I didn’t see the results, but I’ll take his word for it.”

3. “They said the issue is fixed, so I’m taking their word for it.”

4. “I don’t know the exact process, but I’ll take your word for it.”

💡 **Context of usage**:

* In casual talk, it shows trust in someone (e.g., “The food is good? Okay, I’ll take your word for it.”).

* In professional settings, it can sometimes sound a little **doubtful** or **neutral** — like you’re trusting them *for now* but might double-check later.

👉 For formal meetings, you could also say:

* “I’ll trust your judgment on this.”

* “I’ll rely on your assessment.”

### **Alternatives that Show Polite Doubt**

1. **“I’d like to double-check that just to be sure.”**  
    → This sounds cautious, not mistrusting.

2. **“That makes sense, but I think I’ll verify it as well.”**  
    → Shows respect while still confirming.

3. **“I hear you, but I’d prefer to see the details.”**  
    → A softer way of saying you don’t want to just rely on their word.

4. **“I understand your point — can we validate it with data?”**  
    → Very professional, especially in meetings.

### **Spoken Sentences You Can Practice**

* “I’ll take your word for it, but I’d like to confirm with the team.”

* “That sounds convincing; still, let me check the logs.”

* “I trust your input, but I’ll verify it once myself.”

* “That’s helpful — let’s back it up with some numbers to be safe.”

* **Casual**: With friends/family you might just say, *“I’ll take your word for it, but I want to see it myself.”*

* **Professional**: In client calls or team discussions, soften it with phrases like *“just to be safe”* or *“to ensure accuracy”* so it doesn’t sound like you’re doubting their competence.

# Talk in circles

# Talk to vs Talk with

### **✅ Talk to**

* More common in everyday speech.

* Can sometimes sound **one-sided** (like you’re doing most of the talking).

* Example: *“I need to talk to you about the project.”* (I’ll explain something to you).

### **✅ Talk with**

* A bit more **equal and conversational** in tone.

* Suggests a two-way exchange, like a discussion.

* Example: *“I wanted to talk with you about your ideas.”* (we’ll both share thoughts).

### **In your sentence**

👉 *“I wanted to talk with you.”* is perfectly correct and sounds polite, maybe a little more formal.  
 👉 *“I wanted to talk to you.”* is slightly more casual and more common in everyday conversation.

So:

* If you’re speaking casually with a friend → **“talk to you.”**

* If you want to sound cooperative or respectful → **“talk with you.”**

[↑ Back to index](#index)

# Tap into

The phrase "tap into" is another versatile one, and it’s all about accessing or drawing from something, usually a resource, potential, or energy that’s already there but not fully used yet. Think of it like turning on a faucet—you’re tapping into a flow that’s waiting to be unleashed. It’s casual but carries a sense of intentionality, like you’re plugging into something valuable.

Meaning  
At its core, "tap into" means to connect with, use, or take advantage of something—whether it’s a skill, an emotion, a trend, or even a hidden strength. It’s about reaching in and pulling out what’s useful or powerful.

When It’s Used in Speaking  
You’ll hear "tap into" in everyday chats when someone’s talking about unlocking potential, leveraging something, or getting in on a vibe. It’s common in creative, motivational, or practical contexts. Here’s how it shows up:

Accessing Potential or Talent:  
“She tapped into her artistic side and started painting again.”  
(She drew from a skill or passion she hadn’t used much.)  
Using a Resource or Opportunity:  
“We need to tap into the local market to grow the business.”  
(They’re planning to use an available audience or network.)  
Connecting with Emotions or Energy:  
“He tapped into his anger to push through the last round of the fight.”  
(He channeled an emotion to fuel his effort.)  
Catching a Trend or Vibe:  
“The brand tapped into the retro trend and sales took off.”  
(They hooked into something popular and made it work.)  
How It Differs from "Come Through"  
While "come through" is about delivering or succeeding in the moment (like your fight example), "tap into" is more about the process of reaching for something deeper to make that success happen. For instance:

“He came through in the fight” \= He won or held his own.  
“He tapped into his strength during the fight” \= He accessed his inner power to make it happen.  
Why It’s Cool to Use  
It’s got a dynamic feel—like you’re actively grabbing something and putting it to work. People use it to sound insightful or strategic, whether they’re hyping someone up or brainstorming a plan.

Examples in Your World  
Since you’re thinking about starting a company and wrestling with procrastination, you might say:

“I need to tap into my motivation to get this started.”  
(You’re looking to draw from your drive.)  
Or:  
“I could tap into my impulsive energy and just start small.”  
(You’re using a trait you already have in a new way.)

**Meaning:** To access or make use of something (like energy, skills, or emotions).

🗣 *“We need to tap into our creative potential.”*  
 🗣 *“She tapped into her past experiences to write that book.”*

# thrust upon us

### **✅ Meaning of "thrust upon us":**

**"Thrust upon us"** means something was **forced on us suddenly**, **without our choice**, and often **unwelcome**.  
 It implies **lack of control** — the situation, responsibility, or burden was **imposed** rather than chosen.

### **🔹 In simple terms:**

*“We didn’t ask for it — it was pushed on us, and we had to deal with it.”*

### **✅ Example sentences:**

* *The responsibility of leading the project was **thrust upon me** unexpectedly.*

* *War was **thrust upon the nation**, leaving them with no time to prepare.*

* *Change was **thrust upon us**, whether we liked it or not.*

* *After his father’s death, leadership was **thrust upon him** at a young age.*

### **✅ Common Collocations:**

| Phrase | Example |
| ----- | ----- |
| **responsibility thrust upon us** | *The task was suddenly thrust upon us without warning.* |
| **burden thrust upon someone** | *That kind of burden should never be thrust upon a child.* |
| **role thrust upon** | *He had a leadership role thrust upon him.* |

# Ties back

The phrase **“ties back”** is usually used when you want to show a connection between one thing and another. It means *to connect, relate, or link something to something else*.

For example:

* “This discussion ties back to what we were saying yesterday.” → It connects with the earlier discussion.

* “The error ties back to the missing configuration.” → The root cause is linked to that missing configuration.

* “His success ties back to the hard work he put in.” → His success is related to his effort.

### **Phrases you can practice in real speech:**

1. “That really ties back to the main point we were discussing.”

2. “This ties back to our original problem statement.”

3. “On the other hand, it also ties back to the business requirements.”

4. “It all ties back to the way we designed the system.”

It conveys the idea of progress, growth, or change.

* **Example:** Please put away your toys after playing.

2\. To save money

* **Meaning:** To set aside money for future use.  
* **Example:** She puts away a portion of her salary every month for emergencies.

3\. To eat or consume a large amount (informal)

* **Meaning:** To eat or drink a lot of something.  
* **Example:** He can really put away a lot of food at dinner\!

4\. To imprison someone

* **Meaning:** To send someone to prison or confinement.  
* **Example:** The thief was put away for five years.

If you'd like, I can provide more examples or help you practice using it in sentences\!

# To vent at someone

"To vent at someone" means to express strong negative emotions, like anger or frustration, directly and often forcefully towards a particular person, essentially releasing those feelings onto them; it implies complaining or expressing discontent in a somewhat aggressive manner towards that individual.   
Example: "After a long day at work, he came home and vented his frustration at his wife about the traffic."

You came here just to vent at me.

"To vent at someone" means to express strong emotions, typically anger, frustration, or annoyance, directly toward that person, often in a heated or unrestrained way. This can happen when someone needs to release their pent-up feelings and directs it at another person, even if the person may not be the source of the frustration.

Casual Context:  
"She was so stressed at work that she vented at her friend over something trivial."  
Workplace Context:  
"The manager vented at the team after the project missed the deadline, even though it wasn’t entirely their fault."  
Everyday Life:  
"He came home and vented at his family because his car broke down on the way."

# Touch upon

To **mention briefly** or **deal with a subject lightly** without going into full detail.  
 It’s like giving a quick reference to something rather than an in-depth discussion.

### **Usage in sentences**

* *The teacher touched upon the topic of climate change but didn’t go into detail.*

* *The book touches upon issues of identity and belonging.*

* *We only touched upon that subject in the meeting; we’ll discuss it properly tomorrow.*

### **In spoken English**

It’s often used in **formal or semi-formal** situations:

* *“Let me just touch upon a few key points before we end the session.”*

* *“He touched upon his childhood experiences in the interview.”*

✅ **Key idea:** *Touch upon* \= **mention briefly / lightly discuss**.  
 It’s softer than *discuss* or *talk about*.

* **Example (verb):** She **tousled** his hair affectionately.

* **Example (noun):** His hair was in a **tousle** after the nap.

### **🧩 2\. Unkempt (adjective)**

* **Meaning:** **Untidy**, **messy**, or **neglected** in appearance.

* **Example:** He looked **unkempt** after sleeping outdoors.

# Trailblazer

The word **“trailblazer”** is a **noun** that means someone who is the **first to do something** or who **leads the way** in a new field, making it easier for others to follow. It comes from the literal idea of blazing a trail through a forest so others can walk behind you. 🌲✨

✅ **Examples:**

* *Marie Curie was a trailblazer in the field of science.*

* *As the first woman CEO of the company, she was a real trailblazer.*

* *He’s considered a trailblazer in modern architecture.*

👉 In **spoken English**, it’s often used to **praise someone’s courage or innovation**:

* *She’s a trailblazer—always trying new ideas.*

* *That startup was a trailblazer in the electric vehicle market.*

* *He’s not just successful, he’s a trailblazer for future entrepreneurs.*

# Turn Around

To Turn

He turned around the moment I called out his name.

### **Literal meaning**

* To physically rotate your body or change direction.

  * *She turned around to see who was calling her name.*

  * *Turn the car around, we missed the exit.*

### **Figurative meanings in day-to-day English**

1. **To improve or recover from a bad situation**

   * *The company was losing money, but a new CEO turned it around.*

2. **To change opinion/attitude**

   * *At first, he didn’t like the idea, but he turned around after hearing the benefits.*

3. **To complete something quickly (often in business)**

   * *They promised a 24-hour turn-around on repairs.*

   * *The editor turned around the article in just two hours.*  
      → Here, *turnaround* (one word, noun) means **the time taken to finish a task**.

### **Common spoken uses**

* *“Wow, that was a quick turnaround\!”* → something was done really fast.

* *“Don’t worry, things will turn around soon.”* → things will improve.

* *“She really turned her life around.”* → made big positive changes in her life.

✅ So in short:

* **Turn around** (verb) → to change direction, situation, or attitude.

* **Turnaround** (noun) → improvement or the time it takes to complete something.

[↑ Back to index](#index)

# Turn Down

**Meaning:** To reject or refuse.  
 **Example:** *She turned down the job offer.*

[↑ Back to index](#index)

# Turn On

**1\. To Betray or Attack Someone**  
Meaning: To suddenly oppose, betray, or act aggressively toward someone you were previously loyal to.  
Example:  
"I never thought he would turn on his best friend like that."  
"The dog turned on its owner when it felt threatened."  
**2\. To Activate Something**  
Meaning: To switch something on or make it operational.  
Example:  
"Can you turn on the lights, please?"  
"I forgot to turn on the TV before the show started."  
**3\. To Excite or Stimulate**  
Meaning: To arouse interest, enthusiasm, or attraction. (Can be used in general or romantic contexts.)  
Example:  
"Her passion for art really turned me on to painting."  
"He gets turned on by challenges at work."  
**4\. To Depend On (Less Common)**  
Meaning: To rely or be contingent on something.  
Example:  
"The success of the project turns on the team’s ability to meet the deadline."

### **"Turned on" for Hostility:**

* **Structure**: "to turn on \[someone\]" (verb \+ preposition)  
* **Example**: "My friends turned on me." or "She turned on her boss."  
* **Meaning**: To betray, attack, or become hostile toward someone who was previously an ally  
* **Usage**: Always requires an object (the person being betrayed)

### **"Turned on" in Sexual Context:**

* **Structure**: "to be turned on by \[someone/something\]" (passive voice \+ different preposition)  
* **Example**: "I'm turned on by you." or "She was turned on by his confidence."  
* **Meaning**: To be sexually aroused or excited  
* **Usage**: Requires "by" before the source of attraction

### **Key Differences:**

1. **Prepositions**:  
   * Hostility: "turn on \[person\]"  
   * Sexual: "turned on by \[person/thing\]"  
2. **Direction**:  
   * Hostility: The subject is acting against the object  
   * Sexual: The object is causing a reaction in the subject  
3. **Common Phrases**:  
   * Hostility: "He turned on me" (He betrayed me)  
   * Sexual: "He is turned on by me" (I arouse him)

To avoid confusion, make sure you're using the correct preposition ("on" vs. "by") depending on which meaning you intend to convey.

# Turn one’s back

### **Meaning of *Turn one’s back***

1. **Literal (physical action):**

   * Simply facing the other way.

   * *“She turned her back to look out the window.”*

2. **Figurative (ignore, abandon, or reject):**

   * To stop supporting or caring about someone/something.

   * *“He turned his back on his old friends after becoming successful.”*

   * *“The company turned its back on the community’s needs.”*

✅ **Spoken practice sentences:**

* “He turned his back on me when I needed help.”

* “On the other hand, true friends never turn their back on you.”

* “That being said, sometimes you need to turn your back on negativity.”

* “The government shouldn’t turn its back on poor communities.”

👉 **Contexts of usage:**

* **Workplace:** *“We can’t turn our back on customer feedback.”*

* **Personal life:** *“I don’t want to turn my back on my family.”*

* **Casual talk (dramatic style):** *“He just turned his back and walked away.”*

✨ Softening tip: Instead of saying directly *“You turned your back on me”* (which is strong), you can say:

* *“I felt like you weren’t there when I needed you.”*

# Turn Out

**"Turn out"** means:

1. **To happen or end in a certain way** (result).

2. **To come or attend** an event.

### **🗣️ Common Spoken Usage**

#### **1\. Result**

* **"It turned out better than I expected."**  
   → The result was surprisingly good.

* **"The meeting turned out to be useless."**  
   → The result was disappointing.

#### **2\. Attendance**

* **"A lot of people turned out for the concert."**  
   → Many people came.

### **🔁 Summary:**

* **Turn out (result)** → “The exam turned out easy.”

* **Turn out (show up)** → “Only five people turned out for the class.”

# Under advisement

"Under advisement" is a formal phrase that means to consider something carefully or to deliberate on it. For example, you might say "Thank you for your suggestion. We'll take the matter under advisement". 

In legal proceedings, "take under advisement" means a judge will review evidence, consider the issue, and deliberate on it before making a final decision. For example, a court might take a case under advisement when it needs more time to analyze it.

# Undue pressure

**Undue pressure** means **unreasonable, excessive, or unfair pressure** put on someone to make them act in a certain way.  
 It suggests that the force or influence applied is **more than what is normal, acceptable, or legal**.

### **Meaning in simple terms:**

* “Pressure” \= influence or force to make someone do something.

* “Undue” \= not fair, too much, inappropriate.

* Together: **“Unfair or excessive influence that shouldn’t happen.”**

### **Spoken usage examples:**

1. *The manager put undue pressure on the team to finish the project in two days.*

2. *Don’t put undue pressure on yourself—you’ve already done your best.*

3. *He claimed the confession was made under undue pressure from the police.*

4. *Parents sometimes put undue pressure on kids to perform in school.*

💡 **Spoken nuance:**

* In **formal contexts** (law, business, HR), it often implies unethical or illegal pressure.

* In **casual conversation**, it can simply mean “too much stress or expectation.”

[↑ Back to index](#index)

# Unfold

The word **“unfold”** can be used both **literally** and **figuratively**.

### **🔹 Literal meaning → to open up something that was folded**

* *She unfolded the letter and began to read.*

* *He unfolded the map to check the route.*

### **🔹 Figurative meaning → to develop gradually, to reveal, to happen step by step**

* *As the story unfolded, we learned the truth about her past.*

* *The mystery slowly unfolded before the detective’s eyes.*

* *His career is still unfolding—he has so much potential ahead.*

👉 In **spoken English**, it’s often used for **situations, events, or stories** that reveal themselves over time:

* *Let’s wait and see how things unfold.*

* *The evening unfolded better than I expected.*

* *History is unfolding right in front of us.*

# Until afterwards

The phrase **"until afterwards"** means waiting for a certain point in time (or event) to pass before something else happens or is done. It emphasizes that an action or discussion is delayed to take place only after something else has been completed or occurred.

Here are some examples to clarify:

1. **"Let’s wait until afterwards to discuss the details."**  
   (This means the discussion will happen only after a certain event or time.)  
2. **"I didn’t realize the mistake until afterwards."**  
   (This means the mistake was only noticed after the event or situation had already happened.)  
3. **"You won’t fully understand the importance of this decision until afterwards."**  
   (This implies the understanding will come after the consequences or results of the decision are evident.)

[↑ Back to index](#index)

# Unwind

“Unwind” basically means **to relax after being stressed, busy, or tired**.  
 Think of it like loosening a tightly wound spring — you release the tension.

Usage in spoken English:

* After a long day at work:  
   *“I just want to unwind with a good movie.”*

* Talking to friends:  
   *“Let’s go out this weekend and unwind a little.”*

* In casual advice:  
   *“You should take a walk to unwind.”*

### **Common contexts:**

* Work/life balance conversations: *“I usually unwind by listening to music.”*

* Vacation or free time: *“That trip really helped me unwind.”*

* Even in team meetings (light tone): *“After finishing this sprint, we all need some time to unwind.”*

### **Sentences you can practice saying aloud:**

1. *“After a busy day, I like to unwind with some music.”*

2. *“On the other hand, some people unwind by exercising instead of resting.”*

3. *“That being said, I personally prefer to unwind by spending time with my family.”*

4. *“For example, yesterday I unwound by cooking a nice meal.”*

👉 Try speaking these out loud and notice how “unwind” gives a relaxed, easy-going feeling.

**Meaning:** To relax or release tension.  
 **Figurative meaning:** To mentally or emotionally reset.

🗣 *“I like to unwind with a cup of tea after work.”*  
 🗣 *“The weekend is the perfect time to unwind.”*

# Up until this point / Up until now

"Up until this point" means "during the time period before the current moment" \- essentially, it refers to everything that happened leading up to the present time.

It can be used to indicate a change or turning point in a narrative.   
"Up until now" is a common way to use this phrase.

# Walk around

"Walk around" means to move casually or aimlessly in an area, often exploring or inspecting it. It differs from "walk," which simply means to move on foot in a general sense, without implying exploration or a lack of direction.

# Walk through

**1\. To Guide or Explain Step-by-Step**  
To carefully explain or demonstrate a process, procedure, or concept.  
Example: "Can you walk me through the steps to set up the software?"

**2\. A Practice Session or Rehearsal**  
A low-pressure rehearsal, often used in sports, presentations, or performances, to go over details without full intensity.  
Example: "We had a walk-through of the presentation before the big meeting."

**3\. To Inspect or Tour a Place**  
To physically go through a space for examination or familiarization.  
Example: "We did a walk-through of the house before signing the lease."

**4\. To Move Through a Space**  
Simply walking through an area without stopping.  
Example: "He walked through the park on his way home."

# was not the thing yet

the phrase **“was not the thing yet”** is a casual or idiomatic way of saying that *something wasn’t popular, common, or established at that time*.

Let’s break it down 👇

### **🔹 Meaning**

When someone says:

“X was not the thing yet,”  
 they mean that *X wasn’t trendy, known, or widely used at that time.*

It’s like saying:

* “X hadn’t become popular yet.”

* “X didn’t exist or wasn’t mainstream yet.”

### **🔹 Examples in Different Scenarios**

1. **Technology context**

   * “When I started programming, *AI was not the thing yet.* People were still focused on web development.”  
      👉 Meaning: AI wasn’t as popular or mainstream as it is today.

2. **Cultural context**

   * “Back in the early 2000s, *social media was not the thing yet.* We used to chat on MSN Messenger.”  
      👉 Meaning: Social media hadn’t become a trend yet.

3. **Fashion context**

   * “When I was in college, *streetwear was not the thing yet.* Everyone dressed quite formally.”  
      👉 Meaning: Streetwear wasn’t fashionable or widely accepted then.

4. **Workplace context**

   * “Remote work *was not the thing yet* before COVID-19.”  
      👉 Meaning: People didn’t commonly work from home before the pandemic.

### **🔹 Common Spoken Alternatives**

Here are some other phrases you can use with the same idea:

* “That wasn’t popular back then.”

* “It hadn’t caught on yet.”

* “That trend hadn’t started yet.”

* “It wasn’t mainstream at the time.”

### **🔹 Practice Sentences (for speaking)**

Try saying these aloud to get comfortable:

1. “Cloud computing *was not the thing yet* when I started my career.”

2. “Streaming services *were not the thing yet* — we used to buy DVDs.”

3. “Data science *hadn’t caught on yet* when I graduated.”

4. “Back then, working remotely *wasn’t a thing.*”

🗣️ **Tip:** Practice saying it with a natural tone — the phrase “not the thing yet” usually carries a *nostalgic* or *reflective* tone.

# way out

"It was a way out" typically means it was a solution, escape, or means of avoiding a difficult situation.

1\. Literal Escape  
The secret door in the basement wasn't just a myth—it was a way out of the castle.  
When the fire broke out, the window was the only way out.  
2\. Emotional or Psychological Relief  
Talking to a therapist felt like a way out of the overwhelming stress.  
For her, writing poetry was a way out of her sadness.  
3\. Financial or Career Solution  
The new job offer wasn’t ideal, but it was a way out of the debt she had accumulated.  
Learning new skills seemed like a way out of his stagnant career.  
4\. Metaphorical Usage  
Joining the community center gave him a sense of purpose—it was a way out of his loneliness.  
For many, education is a way out of poverty.

# Weigh In

**"Weigh in"** is a phrasal verb that means **to give your opinion, advice, or input on a matter** — especially when a discussion or decision is happening.

### **Meaning in spoken English**

* **Literal origin:** from boxing or sports, where athletes are weighed before competing.

* **Figurative modern meaning:** *join the conversation and share your thoughts or judgment.*

### **Examples**

1. *"We’re deciding which framework to use — can you weigh in?"*

2. *"I’d like to weigh in on the design before we finalize it."*

3. *"Several experts weighed in on the debate."*

💡 **Workplace use:**

* Often used in meetings or group chats when you want someone’s **technical opinion**.

* Can be polite: *"Feel free to weigh in if you have suggestions."*

* Or assertive: *"I want to weigh in here — I think we should refactor the code."*

# Work on

* It means **to focus your effort on something**, usually to improve it, develop it, or complete it.

* It’s the **standard, modern, natural phrase** in both casual and professional English.

* Example: *“I’m working on a new presentation.”* (You’re preparing it.)

* Example: *“She’s working on her English.”* (She’s improving her English skills.)

So whenever you want to talk about tasks, skills, projects, or self-improvement → use **work on**.

### **❌ Why not “work upon”?**

1. **Old-fashioned / Formal** – “Work upon” sounds like something from the 18th or 19th century. Modern English has shifted to “work on.”

   * Example: *“The preacher’s words worked upon their emotions.”* (literary, not everyday speech).

2. **Different nuance** – “Work upon” often means **to influence or have an effect on someone**, not literally doing a task.

   * Example: *“The idea worked upon his mind until he finally acted.”*

3. **Unnatural in conversation** – If you say *“I’m working upon a project”* in a meeting, it will sound odd or too formal. Native speakers almost always say *“working on a project.”*

👉 In short: **use “work on” for everyday tasks/projects/skills.** Reserve “work upon” only if you’re writing old-style literature or want to sound poetic.

### **Spoken examples you can practice:**

1. *“I’m working on a new dashboard design for the client.”*

2. *“On the other hand, I also want to work on my public speaking skills.”*

3. *“That being said, I’m still working on fixing the issue.”*

4. *“Let’s work on this problem together and find a solution.”*

💡 **Tip for meetings:** Whenever you’re tempted to say *“work upon”*, just replace it with *“work on”*. That’s what sounds natural in modern English.

# Write Off / written off

The phrase "written off" has various meanings depending on the context:

Financial Context:

Meaning: To cancel a debt because it is unlikely to be paid or recoverable.  
Example: "The bank wrote off the loan as bad debt after repeated defaults."  
Insurance Context:

Meaning: When an asset, such as a car, is deemed damaged beyond repair or its repair cost exceeds its value.  
Example: "The car was written off after the accident because the repairs were too expensive."  
General Context:

Meaning: To disregard or dismiss someone or something as unimportant, hopeless, or a failure.  
Example: "He was written off as a failure, but he proved everyone wrong by succeeding in his career."

# yank out

The phrase **“yank out”** means to pull something out suddenly and forcefully.  
 It’s informal and often used when the action is quick, strong, or even a bit rough.

3. *“She yanked out her phone to show me the picture.”*

4. *“The machine stopped working when someone yanked out the cable.”*

👉 You can practice saying these aloud.  
 By the way, in professional or polite conversation, instead of *“yank out,”* people often say **“pull out,” “unplug,” or “remove.”**

# Zero Sum Game

A Zero-Sum Game is a situation in which one person's gain is exactly balanced by another person's loss. The total amount of value remains the same—one wins, the other loses.

**Examples**:  
Poker: If one player wins $100, another player (or players) must have lost $100 in total.  
Stock Trading: If a trader profits from a short sale, someone else on the other side of the trade takes the loss.  
**Non-Zero-Sum Example:**  
Business Collaboration: Two companies partnering to create a new product benefit both, instead of one winning at the other’s expense.

## Zero-sum

**adjective**  
relating to or denoting a situation in which whatever is gained by one side is lost by the other.  
"altruism is not a zero-sum game"

# “overspill” and “spill over”

Both **“overspill”** and **“spill over”** can be used with water, but they are used in *different ways* and are *not interchangeable*.

Let me explain clearly and simply.

## **✅ Correct Usage**

### **1\. Spill over (phrasal verb)**

Meaning:  
 Water flows over the edge because the container is full.

**Used for action (verb).**

**Examples (water context):**

* “The tank started to **spill over** because the inlet valve was stuck.”  
* “If you keep filling the bucket, the water will **spill over** the rim.”  
* “The river **spilled over** into the nearby fields due to heavy rains.”

### **2\. Overspill (noun)**

Meaning:  
 The extra amount of water that has spilled.

**Used as a noun (thing).**

**Examples:**

* “There was a lot of **overspill** from the tank.”  
* “The drainage system handles the water **overspill** during monsoon.”

You **don’t say**: “The water overspilled.” (Incorrect as a verb)

## **⭐ Simple rule to remember**

* **Action \= spill over** (verb)  
* **Extra water \= overspill** (noun)

So in conversation, you’ll most often use **spill over**.

## **🗣️ Useful spoken sentences you can practice**

Try saying these aloud:

1. “The tank was almost full, and the water began to **spill over**, so I switched off the pump.”  
2. “During the storm, the lake **spilled over** into the nearby pathway.”  
3. “We need a better drainage system to handle the **overspill**.”  
4. “On the other hand, if we manage the flow properly, we can avoid any **spill over**.”  
5. “For example, open reservoirs often **spill over** after continuous rainfall.”

## **🎤 Practice Tip**

Speak these sentences slowly first, then again with natural speed. Focus on connecting words like *“on the other hand,” “for example,”* etc. These make your speech smoother.

# “reflect on” and “reflect upon”

Both **“reflect on”** and **“reflect upon”** are correct English, and in most situations they mean the **same thing**. The difference is more about **style and tone**, not meaning.

## **Reflect on**

✅ **More common in modern, everyday English**  
 ✅ Sounds **natural, simple, and conversational**  
 ✅ Preferred in both **spoken English** and **professional writing**

**Examples**

* *I need some time to **reflect on** what you said.*  
* *She reflected on her past experiences.*  
* *Let’s reflect on today’s meeting.*

👉 This is the form you should **default to**.

## **Reflect upon**

✅ Slightly **more formal or literary**  
 ✅ Often used in **academic writing, essays, or philosophical contexts**  
 ❌ Less common in daily speech

* *He reflected upon the meaning of success.*  
* *The author reflects upon human suffering.*

👉 Sounds more **old-fashioned or thoughtful**, but not wrong.

## **Do native speakers distinguish them?**

Not really. Native speakers **don’t consciously choose** one for meaning—only for **tone**.

## **Quick rule (easy to remember)**

* **Speaking / meetings / emails** → **reflect on**  
* **Essays / philosophy / literature** → **reflect upon**

### **💡 Since you’re actively improving your spoken English:**

Use **“reflect on”** in conversation.  
 It will sound **natural, fluent, and native-like**.

[↑ Back to index](#index)

# “TL;DR” means “Too Long; Didn’t Read.”

It's an internet shorthand people use when a text, email, or article is very long, and someone wants to give a **short summary** of it. You’ll often see it at the end of a post, where the writer gives a quick version of the main point.

For example:

* *“I wrote a long blog about my trip. TL;DR: It was amazing, but exhausting.”*

* *“TL;DR: The project deadline is extended by one week.”*

### **Phrases you can practice in real conversations:**

1. *“The article was too long; TL;DR, the new policy starts next month.”*

2. *“I’ll give you the TL;DR: we need to focus on automation first.”*

3. *“TL;DR, the meeting went well and we got approval.”*

4. *“That was a lot of information; can you give me the TL;DR version?”*

👉 Context of usage:

* **Workplace:** summarizing long reports or emails.

* **Casual talk:** when explaining a long story quickly.

* **Online forums/chats:** when replying to long posts.

✨ I’d suggest practicing saying: *“TL;DR means a short summary.”* Try saying it aloud as if you’re explaining it to a colleague.

[↑ Back to index](#index)

# “We have other things to do.”

Still, in everyday conversation, especially in American English, people often say **“We got other things to do”** to mean the same thing—it just sounds more relaxed and less formal.

You’d use it when you want to politely cut something short or shift focus, for example:

* Wrapping up a meeting

* Telling a friend you can’t waste time on something trivial

* Indicating you need to move on to another task

✅ Here are some natural sentences you can practice:

1. *“Let’s wrap this up, we got other things to do.”*

2. *“I wish I could stay longer, but I got other things to do.”*

3. *“We can’t spend all day on this, we got other things to do.”*

4. *“It’s been great talking, but I got other things to do right now.”*

👉 Notice how I used connecting phrases like *“but”* and *“let’s wrap this up”* to make the sentences flow.

[↑ Back to index](#index)

# 🔹 Other Contexts Where It’s Used

* Emotions: fear, doubt, jealousy, anxiety  
* Business: risk, inefficiency, delays  
* Technology: bugs, latency, data corruption  
* Health: infection, fatigue  
* Relationships: misunderstandings

## **💡 Small Pronunciation Tip**

It’s pronounced:

**“kreep in”** (long “ee” sound)

Not “crip in.”

## **1\. “Let me catch you up”**

### **✅ Meaning**

It means: *“Let me update you on what has happened so far.”*

👉 Very common in meetings when someone joined late or missed context.

### **🔧 Slight improvement**

Your sentence is correct, but more natural:

* **“Let me catch you up on this.”**  
* **“Let me quickly catch you up.”**

### **💬 Example**

* “Let me catch you up — we already deployed the first version, but we’re facing some issues in RAE.”

### **🗣 Practice Sentences**

* “Let me catch you up on the current status.”  
* “Before we move ahead, let me catch you up.”  
* “Just to bring you up to speed, here’s what happened.”

## **2\. “I have a rudimentary question”**

### **⚠️ Correction**

You wrote: *“rudamentary”* → correct spelling is **rudimentary**

It means: *a very basic or fundamental question*

👉 But in spoken English, this can sound slightly formal or even self-diminishing.

### **👍 Better alternatives (more natural in meetings)**

* “I have a basic question”  
* “This might be a simple question, but…”  
* “Just a quick question…”

* “I have a basic question — how is RAE triggered in this flow?”

* “This might be a simple question, but can you clarify this part?”  
* “I just have a quick question here.”  
* “Sorry if this sounds basic, but I want to confirm something.”

## **3\. “In regards to RAE or in the first place?”**

### **⚠️ Needs restructuring (this is not very natural as is)**

### **What you *probably* want to say:**

You are trying to compare:

* Is this related to RAE?  
* Or was it like this from the beginning?

### **✅ Better natural versions:**

* “Is this specific to RAE, or was it like this from the beginning?”  
* “Is this happening because of RAE, or was it always like this?”  
* “Is this related to RAE, or is it a general behavior?”

* “Is this issue tied to RAE, or does it exist independently?”  
* “On the other hand, was this already the case earlier?”  
* “For example, was this behavior present before RAE changes?”

## **4\. “Has this always been off?”**

### **⚠️ Slight improvement needed**

“off” is vague unless context is very clear.

### **✅ Better versions:**

* “Has this always been disabled?”  
* “Was this always turned off?”  
* “Has this been off from the beginning?”

* “Has this always been disabled, or was it changed recently?”

* “Was this setting always off?”  
* “As far as I remember, this was enabled earlier — did something change?”  
* “That being said, has this been off all along?”

## **5\. “Let’s switch over to this topic”**

Changing focus to another topic

### **👍 More natural options:**

* “Let’s switch to this topic.” (more common)  
* “Let’s move on to this.”  
* “Let’s shift focus to this.”

* “Let’s switch to this topic — the RAE execution flow.”

* “Now, let’s move on to the next point.”  
* “Coming back to this topic…”  
* “Alright, let’s shift focus here.”

## **🔥 Combined Natural Flow (Very Useful for Meetings)**

Try saying this aloud:

“Let me quickly catch you up. We’ve already processed the batches.  
 Now, I have a basic question — is this behavior specific to RAE, or was it always like this?  
 That being said, has this been disabled from the beginning?  
 Anyway, let’s switch to the next topic.”

## **🎯 Speaking Tip**

You already think clearly — your challenge is **structuring thoughts smoothly**.

👉 Focus on connectors:

* “Now,”  
* “That being said,”  
* “On the other hand,”  
* “Just to clarify,”

## **✅ Small Correction in Your Original Sentence**

Your sentence:

“i have rudamentary question”

Better:  
 👉 **“I have a rudimentary question.”**  
 👉 Even better (spoken): **“I have a quick/basic question.”**

## **💪 Practice Task (Important)**

Say this out loud 2–3 times:

“Let me catch you up first. Then I have a quick question — is this specific to RAE, or was it always like this?”

If you want, next step we can do:  
 👉 I’ll simulate a **real meeting**, and you try speaking these naturally.

## **1\. “It never made it way to production.”**

👉 It should be:  
 **“It never made its way to production.”**

Something was planned or developed, but **never got deployed/released**.

### **💬 Natural usage**

* “The fix was ready, but it never made its way to production.”

* “This change never made it to production.”  
* “For some reason, it didn’t go live.”  
* “It was implemented, but it never got deployed.”

## **2\. “Where are you at?”**

This means:

* What’s your current **status/progress**?  
* Where are you in the **process**?

👉 Very common in informal meetings.

### **👍 Slightly more professional options**

* “Where are you with this?”  
* “What’s the current status?”  
* “Can you share where things stand?”

* “Where are you with the debugging so far?”  
* “Can you give a quick status update?”  
* “As of now, where do we stand on this issue?”

## **3\. “You are fragmented”**

### **⚠️ This sounds unnatural in spoken English**

People don’t usually say this directly.

### **What you *mean*:**

The explanation is:

* unclear  
* scattered  
* not structured

### **✅ Better ways to say it:**

* “I think the explanation is a bit fragmented.”  
* “Can you walk me through it more clearly?”  
* “I’m not fully following — could you structure it a bit?”

👉 Softer and more professional tone.

* “I feel we’re jumping between points a bit.”  
* “Could you walk through it step by step?”  
* “Sorry, I’m not fully following — can you clarify?”

## **4\. “Narrowed it down”**

You reduced possibilities and **identified the likely cause**.

👉 Very common in debugging discussions.

* “We’ve narrowed it down to the RAE trigger logic.”

* “We’ve narrowed it down to two possible issues.”  
* “After checking the logs, we narrowed it down further.”  
* “So far, it looks like the problem is in this module.”

## **🔥 Natural Meeting Flow (Combine All)**

Practice saying this:

“It looks like this never made its way to production.  
 Where are you with the investigation so far?  
 I’m not fully following — could you walk me through it step by step?  
 That being said, have we narrowed it down to a specific issue?”

## **🎯 Key Speaking Tip (Important for You)**

Your thoughts are correct, but:  
 👉 Sometimes you sound **too direct or incomplete**

To improve:

* Add softeners: *“I think,” “maybe,” “could you…”*  
* Add connectors: *“that being said,” “so far,” “for example”*

## **💪 Practice Task**

Say this aloud:

“We’ve narrowed it down, but I want to confirm one thing — did this ever make it to production?”

# 🗣 Spoken English Practice (Use in Meetings)

Here are some natural sentences you can practice aloud:

1. “At first everything looked fine, but over time, some issues started creeping in.”  
2. “If we’re not careful, scope creep can creep in during the later phases.”  
3. “Initially the model was stable; however, bias started creeping in after retraining.”  
4. “That being said, small errors can creep in when we scale the pipeline.”  
5. “On the other hand, if we automate validation, these problems won’t creep in.”

Practice saying these slowly and clearly. Focus on natural pauses.
